CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Introduction
The healthcare sector remains a critical pillar of South 
Africa’s social and economic development. Access to 
quality, affordable healthcare not only improves health 
outcomes but also contributes to the well-being, 
productivity, and resilience of communities. Within this 
context, medical schemes and their stakeholders continue 
to play an important role in extending access to healthcare 
for millions of South Africans.
As the regulator of the medical schemes industry, the 
Council for Medical Schemes (CMS) remains committed 
to protecting the interests of members and beneficiaries. 
It is dedicated to promoting a healthcare funding 
environment that is fair, transparent, sustainable, and 
responsive to the needs of those it serves. During the 
year under review, the industry continued to demonstrate 
resilience despite affordability pressures, rising healthcare 
costs, increasing utilisation demands, and broader 
economic challenges affecting households across the 
country.
The CMS oversees 70 medical schemes, comprising 16 
open and 54 restricted schemes, covering approximately 
9.17 million beneficiaries. These beneficiaries are served 
by 36 administrators, 42 managed care organisations, 7 
793 accredited individual brokers and 2 182 accredited 
brokerages. Despite a challenging operating environment, 
the industry remains financially sound, recording a 
solvency ratio of 40.87% in 2024, significantly above the 
statutory minimum requirement of 25%.
The 2025/26 financial year was characterised by significant 
developments in healthcare policy and regulation. These 
included continued progress towards health system 
reform; important milestones in implementing the 
recommendations of the Section 59 Investigation; 
advancement of the Fraud, Waste, Abuse and Error (FWAE) 
framework, and ongoing efforts aimed at strengthening 
affordability, transparency, and accountability within the 
medical schemes environment.
Equally important was the continued strengthening and 
stabilisation of the CMS as an institution. The Council 
remained focused on sound governance, organisational 
resilience, regulatory effectiveness, and strategic alignment 
during the first year of implementing the CMS’ 2025–2030 
Strategic Plan. Collectively, these developments reflect an 
organisation and an industry that continue to evolve in 
response to changing expectations of fairness, value, and 
member protection.
Overview of the Strategy and 
Performance of CMS
The year under review marked the first year of 
implementing the CMS’ 2025–2030 Strategic Plan. 
Guided by its vision of becoming an efficient, effective, 
high-quality, agile, and trusted regulator generating 
sustainable stakeholder value, the CMS continued to 
strengthen its regulatory capability, governance systems, 
and stakeholder engagement efforts.
The organisation achieved an overall performance score 
of 94.83% against its planned targets. This performance 
reflects the commitment of Council, Management, and 
employees to delivering on the CMS’ mandate while 
remaining responsive to the needs of members, 
beneficiaries, and industry stakeholders.
The CMS continued to strengthen beneficiary protection 
through its complaints adjudication function, one of the 
most visible expressions of the organisation’s mandate. 
During the year under review, the organisation processed 
10 818 written submissions through its complaints 

16
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
channels. It also responded to 8 363 enquiries and 
registered 2 455 formal complaints, representing a 25.1% 
increase compared to the previous financial year. Together 
with matters carried forward from the previous year, the 
CMS investigated a total caseload of 2 911 complaints. 
These efforts demonstrate continued public confidence 
in the CMS as an independent avenue for dispute 
resolution and member protection.
The organisation also continued to strengthen regulatory 
oversight, compliance monitoring, governance, and 
policy capability. Significant progress was made in: 
implementing the recommendations of the Section 59 
Investigation Final Report; advancing the FWAE agenda; 
progressing the review of Prescribed Minimum Benefits 
(PMBs), and strengthening consumer education and 
empowerment initiatives.
The CMS remains committed to sound governance, 
accountability and prudent financial management. During 
the year under review, Council continued to strengthen 
oversight mechanisms, internal controls and governance 
processes to support the effective execution of the 
organisation’s mandate. I am pleased to report that the 
CMS attained a clean audit outcome for the 2025/26 
financial year, reflecting the organisation’s continued 
commitment to good governance, financial discipline, 
transparency and accountability in the management of 
public resources.
The medical schemes industry continued to demonstrate 
resilience despite economic pressures and rising 
healthcare costs. Beneficiary numbers increased from 
approximately 9.13 million in 2023 to 9.17 million in 
2024. While this growth is encouraging, it remains below 
broader population growth. The industry continues to 
face demographic shifts, with an ageing beneficiary 
population contributing to increased healthcare utilisation 
and expenditure.
The total benefits paid increased by 8.52% to R259.3 
billion in 2024, while claims paid per beneficiary increased 
by 7.84%. The PMB expenditure increased by 9.09% to 
R135.66 billion and out-of-pocket expenditure reached 
R46.3 billion. These trends reinforce the importance of 
ongoing efforts to improve affordability, strengthen 
financial protection, and ensure the long-term 
sustainability of healthcare funding arrangements.
Strategic Relationships
The CMS recognises that meaningful and sustainable 
reform can only be achieved through collaboration. 
During the year under review, the organisation continued 
to strengthen its relationships with various role players. 
These included the Ministry of Health; the National 
Department of Health (NDoH); regulatory authorities; 
healthcare practitioners; medical schemes; administrators, 
and other key stakeholders in support of its regulatory 
mandate and broader healthcare reform objectives.
Particular emphasis was placed on dialogue and 
consultation relating to the implementation of the 
recommendations of the Section 59 Investigation Final 
Report. Through ongoing engagements with healthcare 
practitioners, professional associations, medical schemes, 
and administrators, the CMS sought to ensure that 
implementation processes remain balanced, transparent, 
and responsive to stakeholder concerns.

COUNCIL TENURE HIGHLIGHTS
18
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
The CMS also provided transitional guidance to the 
industry while broader legislative and policy reforms 
continue to be explored.
The CMS further supported the work of advisory and 
collaborative structures established to advance key 
regulatory priorities. These included stakeholder 
platforms relating to the Fraud, Waste, Abuse and Error 
(FWAE) framework, as well as advisory structures 
supporting the review of Prescribed Minimum Benefits 
(PMBs). The engagements with these platforms and 
structures continue to play an important role in ensuring 
that regulatory interventions are informed by technical 
expertise, stakeholder perspectives, and evidence-based 
policy considerations.
The CMS Industry Indaba represented an important 
milestone in strengthening dialogue, trust, and 
cooperation across the healthcare funding sector. The 
platform enabled regulators, medical schemes, 
administrators, healthcare practitioners, policymakers, 
and other stakeholders to engage constructively. They 
deliberated on matters relating to the implementation of 
the Section 59 recommendations, the future direction of 
the FWAE framework, healthcare affordability, value-
based care, and the broader health system reform. 
Among the key outcomes was stakeholder support for 
the inclusion of  ‘Error’ within the FWAE framework. This 
recognised the need for a more balanced and 
proportionate approach to addressing irregular claims 
and payment practices across the healthcare funding 
environment.
The CMS also strengthened its commitment to evidence-
based regulation through the Research Symposium, 
which brought together researchers, policymakers, 
regulators, and industry stakeholders to engage on 
emerging healthcare funding issues. The Symposium 
forms part of a broader strategy aimed at strengthening 
the use of research and evidence in regulatory decision-
making and policy development. This commitment was 
further reinforced through ongoing work towards 
establishing a peer-reviewed journal focused on medical 
schemes, healthcare financing, and health policy research.
Strategic partnerships remain an important enabler of the 
CMS’ work. 
During the year under review, the organisation maintained 
and concluded five Memoranda of Understanding, 
exceeding its annual target. These partnerships support 
coordinated approaches to regulation, research, capacity 
building, stakeholder engagement, and the resolution of 
systemic challenges within the healthcare funding 
environment.
The CMS also continued to strengthen its longstanding 
partnership with the Committee of Insurance, Securities 
and Non-Bank Financial Authorities (CISNA). The latter 
provides an important platform for regional collaboration, 
knowledge sharing, and engagement on social health 
protection and healthcare financing matters. Through this 
partnership, the CMS contributes to and benefits from 
broader African and international perspectives on 
healthcare regulation, governance, and beneficiary 
protection.
Beyond supporting regulatory cooperation, these 
partnerships have enhanced the CMS’ ability to convene 
stakeholders, facilitate dialogue, and advance meaningful 
reform. Strategic relationships continue to play a critical 
role in strengthening the effectiveness, relevance, and 
impact of the CMS as a regulator. This applies to situations 
relating to: the implementation of the Section 59 
recommendations; the advancement of the FWAE 
framework; collaboration on PMB reform; participation in 
regional forums such as CISNA, and the promotion of 
evidence-based regulation.
Challenges faced by the Council
The Council remains acutely aware of the challenges 
facing both the healthcare funding sector and the CMS as 
a regulator.
Healthcare affordability remains one of the most 
significant challenges confronting the industry. Rising 
healthcare costs, economic pressures, and constrained 
household incomes continue to place pressure on medical 
scheme affordability and long-term membership growth.
The CMS also continues to operate within an evolving 
and increasingly complex policy environment. Ongoing 
developments relating to National Health Insurance, 
healthcare financing reform, and broader legislative 
changes require the organisation to remain agile and 
responsive while maintaining regulatory certainty and 
protecting beneficiaries.
The sustainability of the CMS’ funding model remains an 
important strategic consideration. During the year under 
review, progress was made towards reviewing the 
organisation’s funding model through the development 
of Terms of Reference for a comprehensive funding model 
review. The Terms of Reference were submitted to the 
National Department of Health and shared with National 
Treasury and are currently undergoing approval processes.
The Council remains committed to ensuring that the CMS 
is adequately resourced to fulfil its expanding regulatory 
responsibilities. This includes reviewing the current levy 
dispensation, exploring alternative revenue-generation 
mechanisms, and ensuring that the organisation remains 
financially sustainable with sufficient reserves to respond 
to emerging regulatory priorities.
Management continues to pursue expenditure 
optimisation initiatives aimed at improving operational 
efficiency and ensuring value for money. These include 
strengthening internal capacity to manage labour-related 
matters and implementing governance mechanisms to 
assess legal matters before external legal processes are 
pursued.
The organisation also continues to face technology 
modernisation requirements and capacity constraints 
associated with an expanding regulatory mandate and 
increasing stakeholder expectations.
19
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Strategic Focus 
Looking ahead, the Council remains focused on 
positioning the CMS as a trusted and future-ready 
regulator capable of supporting healthcare reform while 
safeguarding the interests of beneficiaries.
A key area of focus will be supporting the implementation 
of National Health Insurance and contributing towards 
the achievement of Universal Health Coverage. The CMS 
will continue to provide technical, regulatory, and policy 
support while assisting the industry in navigating the 
transition towards a reformed healthcare system.
The review of PMBs and the development of a fit-for-
purpose Primary Healthcare Package will remain 
important priorities to ensure that benefit structures 
remain relevant, sustainable, and aligned with national 
health priorities.
The CMS will continue to drive implementation of the 
Section 59 recommendations through stakeholder 
engagement, legislative review, and policy development 
processes. 
Following stakeholder support for the inclusion of  ‘Error’  
within the FWAE framework, the CMS will continue to 
advance the implementation of the FWAE agenda. This 
will happen through: the renewal of the Charter; the 
refinement of the Codes of Good Practice; the 
development of Standard Operating Procedures; the 
strengthening of prevention, detection, and recovery 
mechanisms, and further work on Tribunal structures and 
dispute resolution processes. These initiatives will 
contribute towards a more transparent, accountable, and 
equitable healthcare funding environment.
The Council further recognises the importance of tariff 
reform, pricing transparency, and value-based healthcare 
in supporting affordability and sustainability within the 
healthcare sector. 
Digital transformation will remain a strategic priority. The 
CMS is implementing a multi-year programme aimed at 
modernising regulatory, operational, and stakeholder-
facing systems. Key initiatives include enhancements to 
records management, accreditation systems, complaints 
management, research and monitoring capabilities, 
enterprise resource planning systems, learning 
management platforms, and digital stakeholder 
interfaces, including the CMS website. These investments 
will strengthen regulatory effectiveness, improve service 
delivery, and support organisational efficiency.
The Council also supports ongoing efforts to review the 
CMS’ funding model and explore alternative funding 
mechanisms. This will enable the organisation to maintain 
regulatory independence, strengthen institutional 
capability, and respond effectively to future regulatory 
demands.

My gratitude also extends to my fellow Council Members 
and Deputy Chairperson, Dr Honours Mukhari, for their

I would like to thank the Chief Executive and Registrar, Dr 
Musa Gumede, the Executive Management team, and all 
the CMS’ employees for their professionalism, dedication, 
and commitment to advancing the organisation’s 
mandate.
I further acknowledge the contribution of medical 
schemes, administrators, managed care organisations, 
healthcare practitioners, industry associations, co-
regulators, and other strategic partners. They continue to 
engage constructively with the CMS in pursuit of a 
sustainable and equitable healthcare system.
As I reflect on the work of the current Council, I am 
particularly encouraged by the progress made in 
strengthening the organisation’s governance, stability, 
and strategic positioning. This period has required 
difficult decisions, meaningful engagement, and a 
collective commitment to ensuring that the CMS remains 
responsive to the needs of beneficiaries and stakeholders 
alike.
Throughout this Council’s tenure, we have sought to 
strengthen the foundations of the organisation while 
creating space for dialogue on some of the most complex 
and sensitive issues facing the healthcare sector, 
particularly the healthcare funders and their members. 
Important work remains. Nevertheless, I am confident 
that the foundations laid during this period will support 
the CMS in continuing to protect beneficiaries, promote 
fairness, and contribute meaningfully to South Africa’s 
pursuit of Universal Health Coverage.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
General financial review of CMS 
 
The 2025/26 financial year presented a demanding 
operating environment. It was characterised by sustained 
economic pressure, increasing expectations of regulators, 
and continued investment requirements to modernise 
the organisation’s systems and capabilities. Despite these 
challenges, the Council for Medical Schemes (CMS) 
remained focused on executing its statutory 
responsibilities. 
This was realised through prudent financial management, 
disciplined resource allocation, and a careful prioritisation 
of organisational initiatives. Throughout the year, 
management continued to balance immediate operational 
requirements with longer-term investments necessary to 
strengthen regulatory effectiveness and organisational 
sustainability. 
 
The CMS executed its mandate with a budget of R249 
million in the 2025/26 financial year from the following 
sources:  
• Principal scheme member once-off levies (86%)  
• Revenues generated through regulatory activities (4%)  
• Grant from the National Department of Health (3%)  
• Surplus funds rolled over from the 2024/25 financial   
 year (5%)  
• Interest earned and other income (2%)  
 
Although the CMS continues to maintain sound financial 
governance, the organisation is operating within an 
increasingly constrained funding environment. Rising 
operational costs, expanding regulatory responsibilities, 
investment in modern information technology systems, 
and increasing expectations regarding regulatory 
oversight continue to place pressure on available 
resources. These pressures reinforce the importance of 
ensuring that every allocation directly contributes to 
achieving the organisation’s strategic objectives and to 
the effective regulation of the medical schemes industry. 
 
The CMS’ primary source of revenue is derived from levy 
impositions. Approximately 4 million principal members 
of medical schemes are levied a once-off amount each 
year. The extent of levies imposed each year is subject to 
the Ministers of Health and Finance’s concurrence and 
approval of a proposal made by the CMS. The level of the 
levy imposed on principal members in 2025/26 was 
R51.49 (2024/25: R48.58) per member per annum, 
representing an increase of R2.91 (6%) per principal 
member per annum.  
 
The second stream of income that contributes to the 
budget of the CMS is generated through regulatory 
activities. These are fees charged for registering schemes, 
registering new rules and amendments, registering, 
renewing, and accrediting administrators, managing care 
organisations, and paying brokers. These tariff rates  were 
adjusted for inflation with approval from the Minister of 
Health in the prior financial year. Revenue from this stream 
depends on the applications received. 
 
Additionally, the entity receives a third stream of income 
through grant funding from the National Department of 
Health (NDoH). This grant funding is mainly used for the 
research support provided by the CMS to NDoH 
initiatives. Lastly, the rolled-over surplus funds are 
approved by the National Treasury in terms of s53(3) of 
the Public Finance Management Act (1 of 1999). It relates 
to surplus funds generated by the CMS during the 
previous periods. Once approved, these funds are used 
for specific cost pressures during the new financial year. 
 
From a management perspective, ensuring that the 
organisation remains appropriately resourced is 
21
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
fundamental to sustaining effective regulation. The 
planned review of the CMS funding model during 
2026/27 provides an opportunity to assess whether the 
current funding framework adequately supports the 
operational demands of a modern regulator. The review 
will consider the growing investment required in 
technology, the specialised regulatory capacity, and data-
driven oversight. The purpose is to ensure that the 
organisation remains capable of fulfilling its statutory 
obligations efficiently and effectively. 
 
Spending trends of CMS 
 
In terms of expenditure trends, the CMS reported a lower 
financial loss in the current year compared with the prior 
year. The retention of surplus funds provided financial 
relief and enabled the CMS to meet some of its ICT 
investment requirements. However, increased expenditure 
on personnel and legal fees contributed to a reduction in 
the entity’s net assets.  
 
It is, however, important to note that the entity’s 
compensation of employees’ budget is insufficient to 
fund the organisation’s entire structure. Therefore, the 
entity is phasing in the implementation of its organogram 
based on critical areas and affordability. 
 
Notwithstanding these financial pressures, expenditure 
continued to be directed towards activities that support 
the organisation’s core regulatory mandate. Investment 
remained focused on strengthening governance, 
enhancing consumer protection, improving regulatory 
oversight, and advancing the modernisation of the 
organisation’s ICT environment. This disciplined approach 
to resource allocation reflects the Council’s commitment 
to ensuring that limited financial resources continue to 
generate maximum regulatory value and support 
improved service delivery. 
 
Capacity constraints and challenges
 
The organisation continues to experience increasing 
operational demands across several regulatory functions 
while balancing affordability and available resources. 
Particular pressure remains in specialised technical 
capacity, digital capability, and the information technology 
infrastructure. All of these are essential to supporting 
efficient regulatory processes and improving service 
delivery. Management therefore continues to implement 
organisational improvements in a phased and financially 
sustainable manner, prioritising areas of greatest 
operational risk. 
 
Discontinued key activities 
 
The entity did not discontinue any activities in the 2025/26 
financial year.  
 
New or proposed key activities 
 
The CMS did not have any new activities in the period 
under review. 
Requests for rollover of funds 
 
The entity has reported an accumulated surplus of R316 
000 in the year under review and will be applying to 
National Treasury for approval to retain the funds.  
 
Supply Chain Management 
 
Supply Chain Management (SCM) is a centralised function 
in the Office of the Chief Financial Officer. Compliance 
with National Treasury Regulations and guidelines 
continues to improve. This is demonstrated by no new 
irregular expenditure incurred in the financial year and 
the reduction of the Fruitless and Wasteful Expenditure 
incurred. Additional resources are, however, still needed 
to support the business and to ease pressure on the Unit 
and to further improve internal processes and increase 
efficiencies. Additional resources in the form of interns 
and/or contract employees are contracted in as and when 
required. 
 
a. SCM processes and systems in place
 
The SCM systems and processes are in place and fully 
functional. The CMS has employed the services of 
external consultants, to assist the entity with the 
assessment and determination process of the Irregular, 
Fruitless and Wasteful expenditure (IFWE), and perform 
required investigations, in line with the National Treasury 
Compliance and Reporting Framework. This intervention 
led to finalisation of all legacy IFWE and enabled the 
submission of condonation and/or write-off requests to 
National Treasury and the Council. 
 
During the year under review, continued emphasis was 
placed on strengthening procurement governance and 
improving internal controls. There was also emphasis on 
enhancing compliance with the Public Finance 
Management Act and the National Treasury regulatory 
framework. These improvements contribute not only to 
stronger financial governance but also to greater 
organisational efficiency, accountability, and public 
confidence in the management of public resources. 
 
b. Concluded unsolicited bid proposals for the year 
 
The CMS did not conclude any contracts through the 
unsolicited bid process during the financial year under 
review.

Audit report matters and how they 
would be addressed 
The CMS achieved a clean audit outcome for the 2025/26 
financial year, reflecting the effectiveness of the 
organisation’s financial management, governance, internal 
control environment and financial reporting processes.
Management has already implemented corrective measures 
to strengthen performance planning, monitoring and 
reporting, with the aim of improving the quality, reliability 
and consistency of performance information in future 
reporting cycles.
The organisation also sustained improvements in its 
governance environment by resolving the material 
findings relating to consequence management reported 
in previous years. This demonstrates the continued 
strengthening of internal controls, accountability and 
governance practices across the organisation.
During the year under review, consultants continued to 
support the organisation in resolving legacy irregular 
expenditure and fruitless and wasteful expenditure 
matters dating back to 2014. This intervention enabled 
the finalisation of historical cases and the submission of 
condonation and write-off requests to the relevant 
governance structures in accordance with the National 
Treasury Irregular Expenditure Framework.
While the clean audit outcome represents a significant 
organisational milestone, management remains committed 
to continuously strengthening internal controls, enhancing 
financial and performance reporting, and embedding a 
culture of accountability to sustain this achievement and 
support the effective delivery of the CMS’ regulatory 
mandate.
 
 
Plans to address financial challenges 
 
During the 2026/27 financial year, management will 
continue implementing initiatives aimed at strengthening 
operational efficiency, modernising technology platforms, 
enhancing financial sustainability, and improving 
organisational capability. Particular attention will be given 
to implementing the ICT modernisation programme, 
supporting the review of the funding model, and 
strengthening internal business processes. There will also 
be a focus on ensuring that the available resources 
continue to be directed towards activities that have the 
greatest regulatory impact. 
 
Events after the reporting date 
 
NEHAWU took the CMS to the CCMA for the short 
payment of the 2024/25 performance bonus. In April 
2026, NEHAWU and the CMS resolved to keep the 
arbitration on this matter in abeyance pending the 
settlement agreement negotiations to resolve the matter 
internally. An agreement was reached between the CMS 
and NEHAWU on 15 May 2026. The agreement resulted 
in the non-adjusting event relating to payment of the 
portion of the performance bonus amount initially 
demanded by NEHAWU for 2024/25 financial year. 
 
Economic Viability 
 
Regarding the CMS’ economic viability, the Annual 
Financial Statements have been prepared on a going 
concern basis, as the Council is satisfied that the CMS will 
continue operating for the foreseeable future. Although 
the organisation experienced financial pressures during 
the year under review, its overall financial position remains 
positive. This is supported by prudent financial 
management, robust governance arrangements, and an 
approved strategy to strengthen long-term sustainability. 
 
Also, the Council has approved a comprehensive financial 
sustainability strategy aimed at restoring and 
strengthening the organisation’s financial position over 
the medium term. This includes disciplined expenditure 
management, continued monitoring of cash flow, and 
implementation of efficiency measures. It also involves 
active engagement with the National Department of 
Health regarding the review of the CMS funding model. 
These interventions are intended to ensure that the 
organisation remains financially resilient while continuing 
to deliver on its statutory mandate. 
 
Unfortunately, the CMS’ financial position has declined 
during the current financial year; this was due to the 
reduction of cash and cash equivalents and the utilisation 
of the accumulated surplus. Therefore, the Council has 
approved a turnaround strategy to improve the financial 
position and sustainability of the CMS over the long term. 
 
The Council-approved cash flow projection for 2026/27 
shows a projected surplus of R8 million based on updated 
figures. Budget controls are in place to mitigate the risk 
of overspending. 
 
Legal matters currently underway may result in either 
cash outflows (if lost) or inflows (if successful), as reflected 
in the Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets notes. 
 
On the bright side, there are no plans to liquidate or 
cease operations. The CMS remains a statutory body 
governed by the Medical Schemes Act and primarily 
funded through the Levies Act, with no indication of 
repeal of this law. 
 
The revenue targets for 2025/26 were marginally 
exceeded, and management expects the performance to 
be maintained and improved in 2026/27. The 2026/27 
operations have been fully budgeted for. 
 
As such, management remains committed to building an 
agile, financially sustainable, and digitally enabled 
regulator. Continuous improvement, disciplined financial 
management, investment in organisational capability, 
and a culture of accountability will remain central to the

position the organisation to respond effectively to 
emerging regulatory demands while continuing to deliver 
value to stakeholders and the beneficiaries of medical

The following matter has no material impact on the

The Section 59 Investigation, initiated in 2019, was

Report was submitted to the Minister of Health on 7 July 
2025. During the year under review, the Council continued 
to implement its approved response to the Report 
through structured stakeholder engagement and the 
phased implementation of the recommendations within 
its mandate. 
 
To support this process, the CMS established an internal 
implementation task team responsible for coordinating 
activities, monitoring progress, and facilitating 
collaboration across the organisation. The implementation 
process is being undertaken in close consultation with the 
National Department of Health. This is to ensure 
alignment with broader policy considerations, statutory 
requirements, and the respective responsibilities of the 
various role players. 
 
As implementation of the recommendations is a multi-
year process involving a range of stakeholders, progress 
will continue to be monitored and reported through the 
appropriate governance structures. The CMS remains 
committed to implementing those recommendations 
that fall within its legislative mandate. It is also committed  
to supporting broader reforms aimed at promoting 
fairness, transparency, and accountability within the 
medical schemes environment.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Legislative and Other Mandates 
Legislative mandates
Section 9 of the Constitution of the Republic of South 
Africa (108 of 1996) states that everyone has the right to 
equality, including access to health care services. This 
means that individuals should not be unfairly excluded 
from the provision of health care.
People also have the right to access information that is 
held by another person if it is required for the exercise 
or protection of a right. This may arise in relation to 
accessing one’s medical records from a health facility for 
the purposes of lodging a complaint or for giving 
consent for medical treatment. This enables people to 
exercise their autonomy in decisions related to their 
health, which is an integral part of the rights to human 
dignity and bodily integrity in sections 9 and 12 of the 
Constitution, respectively.
Section 27 of the Constitution places the obligation on 
the State to put reasonable legislation in place to realise 
socio-economic rights, including access to health care 
progressively.
The Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998) (MSA) represents 
such legislation, which creates the framework for non-
discriminatory access to medical schemes. The MSA 
provides for the regulation of the medical schemes 
industry to ensure synchrony and consonance with the 
national health objectives.
Section 27 of Chapter 2 of the Bill of Rights of the 
Constitution states the following with regards to health 
care, food, water, and social security:
Everyone has the right to access to:
• Healthcare services, including reproductive healthcare.
• Sufficient food and water.
• Social security, including appropriate social assistance, 
if they are unable to support themselves and their 
dependents.
The State must provide reasonable legislative and other 
measures within its available resources to achieve the 
progressive realisation of these rights, and no one may 
be refused emergency medical treatment.
Section 36 of the Constitution deals with the limitation 
of rights and it spells out strict criteria which must be 
adhered to whenever the rights included in the Bill of 
Rights are limited by law. Section 22 of the Constitution 
guarantees the freedom of trade, which may be limited 
by law.
The Medical Schemes Act limits the business of a 
medical scheme to those parties registered by the 
Council for Medical Schemes and requires such parties 
to comply with the provision of the Medical Schemes 
Act.
The National Health Act (61 of 2003) (NHA)
The NHA provides the framework for a structured 
uniform health system for our country, considering the 
obligations imposed by the Constitution and other laws 
on the national, provincial, and local governments 
regarding health services. A key objective of the NHA is 
to unite the various elements of the national health 
system to actively promote and improve the  system in 
South Africa. Added to this is the intent to foster a spirit 
of cooperation and shared responsibility among all role 
players. These include public and private health 
professionals, providers, and other relevant stakeholders 
within the context of national, provincial, and district 
health plans.
The Charter for the Public and Private 
Health Sectors of South Africa, 2006
This Health Charter was initiated in support of the NHA. 
It indicates that the public and private health sectors 
need to constructively engage with each other in 
discussions and dialogue to create an improved 
healthcare delivery system for South Africa. Such a 
system will need to be coherent, efficient, cost-effective 
and quality-driven, and optimise the use of both sectors’ 
resources to benefit the entire citizenry.
The Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998)
The Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998) established the 
Council for Medical Schemes (CMS). Section 7 of the 
MSA confers the following functions on the CMS:
• Protect the interests of the beneficiaries at all times.
• Control and coordinate the functioning of medical 
schemes in a manner that is complementary to the 
national health policy.
• Make recommendations to the Minister of Health on 
criteria for the measurement of quality and outcomes 
of the relevant health services provided by medical  
schemes. Also make recommendations on such other 
services as the Council may from time to time  
determine.
• Investigate complaints and settle disputes in relation 
to the affairs of medical schemes as provided for in  
this Act.
• Collect and disseminate information about private 
health care.
• Make rules not inconsistent with the provisions of the 
Act for the purpose of the performance of its functions 
and the exercise of its powers.
• Advise the Minister of Health on any matter concerning 
medical schemes.
• Perform any other functions conferred on the CMS 
by the Minister of Health or the Act.
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26 27
Legislation impacting and influencing the 
functioning of the CMS
Council for Medical Schemes Levy Act (58 of 2000)  –
Provides a legal framework for the Council to collect 
levies from medical schemes.
Public Finance Management Act (1 of 1999 (PFMA))  – 
Provides for the effective, efficient, and economic 
financial management in government departments and 
public entities.
Financial Sector Regulation Act (9 of 2017 (FSRA))  –
Establishes a system of financial regulation by 
establishing the Prudential Authority and the Financial 
Sector Conduct Authority.
National Development Plan Vision 2030
As an organ of the State, the Council is obliged to 
discharge its legislated mandate in a coherent manner 
that is consistent with the national policy, as set out in 
the National Development Plan (NDP) Vision 2030.
The following are key priorities of the NDP Vision 2030 
(extract from Chapter 10):
• Raise the life expectancy of South Africans to at least 
70 years.
• Progressively improve TB prevention and cure.
• Reduce maternal, infant, and child mortality.
• Significantly reduce the prevalence of non-
communicable diseases.
• Reduce injury, accidents, and violence by 50% from  
2010 levels.
• Complete health system reforms.
• Primary healthcare teams provide care to families and 
communities.
• Universal health coverage.
• Fill posts with skilled, committed, and competent 
individuals.
Furthermore, the NDP Vision 2030 sets out nine priority 
areas that highlight the key interventions required to 
achieve a more effective health system to contribute to 
achieving the desired outcomes. The priority areas are:
• Address the social determinants that affect health  
and diseases.
• Strengthen the health system.
• Improve health information systems.
• Prevent and reduce the disease burden and promote 
health.
• Finance universal health care coverage.
• Improve human resources in the health sector.
• Review management positions and appointments,  
and strengthen accountability mechanisms.
• Improve quality by using evidence.
• Meaningful public–private partnerships.
Policy mandates
The political environment has been stable for the greater 
part of this five-year period. The Minister of Health has 
been consistent in articulating policy developments that 
affect the industry. The policy mandate and context for 
the health sector and the medical schemes industry 
have largely been driven by:
• National Development Plan Vision 2030
• Sustainable Development Goals
• Strategic Plan of the National Department of Health 
These policy mandates remain relevant for the medical 
schemes industry for the next five years. It is, however, 
important to note that these mandates are committing 
the health sector (both private and public) to the 
following key deliverables:
• Increased life expectancy.
• Reduction of maternal, infant, and child mortality.
• Reduction in the burden of HIV and TB.
• Reduction in the burden of non-communicable  
diseases, including violence.
• Universal health coverage.

CMS Service Delivery Environment 
This section will pertain to the regulatory scope of the 
CMS. Additionally, a financial overview of the CMS’ 
finances will be given to shed light on its performance 
during 2024. 
Regulatory Scope 
As far as the regulatory scope is concerned, the primary 
function of the CMS is to regulate medical schemes in 
South Africa through the Medical Schemes Act (131 of 
1998) (MSA) and Regulations. The purpose is to ensure 
the protection of the interests of all medical schemes’ 
beneficiaries. This entails ensuring that all regulated 
entities consistently comply with the MSA and its 
provisions.
By the end of March 2026, the medical schemes industry 
comprised of 70 registered schemes; 36 administrators 
(including self-administered schemes and limited 
accreditation administrators); 42 managed care 
organisations (including schemes providing their own 
managed care services); 2 182 broker organisations, and; 
7 793 individual brokers. 
While the absence of further contraction suggests a 
period of structural stability, it also indicates that 
consolidation gains have largely plateaued following a 
long-term decline in the number of schemes from 144  in 
2000. Beneficiary numbers increased from approximately 
9.13 million in 2023 to 9.17 million in 2024, representing 
a year-on-year growth of about 0.45%. 
Although positive, this growth rate remains modest and 
continues to lag behind the population growth. However, 
the beneficiary population is gradually ageing, with the 
average age rising from 34.0 years in 2023 to 34.2 years 
in 2024 (0.6% increase).

is included in the report to management, with material findings being reported under the Predetermined Objectives

AUDITOR’S REPORT: PREDETERMINED OBJECTIVES
33
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Figure 1 shows that while young children under one year 
declined by around 15% between 2018 and 2024, 
adolescents and young adults, especially females aged 
15–19, increased slightly. Adults aged 30–49 remained 
stable, whereas beneficiaries aged 65 and older, 
particularly those 85+, grew sharply by 26% for females 
and 29% for males. Gender differences persist, with 
female beneficiaries having a higher average age than 
male beneficiaries. 
This demographic pattern contributes to sustained 
utilisation growth and reinforces cost pressures, 
particularly in areas such as chronic care, reproductive 
health, and specialist services. 
Membership Dynamics in Restricted and 
Government-Associated Schemes
Membership growth across the South African medical 
schemes industry was not uniform during the reporting 
period. While the overall industry growth remained 
modest, important differences emerged across the 
different types of schemes and individual schemes. 
Restricted schemes, linked to large employers or 
government institutions, generally demonstrated greater 
resilience and stronger membership growth than the 
industry as a whole. These patterns suggest that 
membership dynamics are influenced by institutional 
affiliation, scheme size, and employment stability.
Restricted and government-affiliated schemes continued 
to outperform the broader industry in membership 
growth. Between 2023 and 2024, beneficiaries in 
government-funded schemes increased from 3 169 152 
to 3 290 886, representing a growth rate of 3.8%, 
substantially higher than the industry average. The 
Government Employees Medical Scheme (GEMS) 
recorded a 5.2% increase in membership, consolidating 
its dominant position within the restricted scheme 
environment. Other schemes, such as LA-Health, 
experienced even stronger relative growth of 6.7%, while 
several smaller schemes reported increases of 1.5% to 
2.9%. In contrast, a subset of restricted schemes 
experienced sharp membership declines. Medipos 
contracted by 36.6%, while Transmed declined by 14.2%. 
These contrasting trends illustrate widening divergence 
within the restricted scheme landscape, where scale and 
employment-linked stability increasingly differentiate 
growth trajectories.
Healthcare Expenditure and Benefits Paid
Healthcare expenditure increased between 2023 and 
2024. The total benefits paid rose from R239.0 billion in 
2023 to R259.3 billion in 2024, an 8.52% year-on-year 
increase. This growth rate exceeded both general inflation 
and average wage growth, reinforcing the structural 
mismatch between healthcare cost escalation and 
household affordability. On a per-beneficiary basis, claims 
paid per average beneficiary per annum (PABPA) 
increased by 7.84%, rising from R26 404.69 in 2023 to 
R28 474.15 in 2024. Risk benefits paid per beneficiary 
increased even more sharply, by 8.71%, indicating 
growing pressure on pooled funds. The distribution of 
expenditure remained broadly stable between the two 
years. Hospital services accounted for 35.95% of total 
benefits paid, while specialist services represented 
28.02%. Medicines dispensed constituted 14.05%, with 
supplementary and allied health professionals accounting 
for 8.47%. The consistency of this pattern suggests that 
cost pressures are systemic rather than driven by isolated 
service categories.
Out-of-Pocket Payments
Out-of-pocket payments continued to rise between 2023 
and 2024, reflecting both higher utilisation and persistent 
gaps between provider charges and scheme 
reimbursements. At an industry-wide level, total out-of-
pocket expenditure increased at an average annual rate 
of 6.09% over the long term, reaching R46.3 billion in 
2024. Between 2023 and 2024, medicines dispensed 
remained the largest contributor to out-of-pocket costs, 
accounting for 35.09%, while payments to specialists 
increased from 27.59% to 28.47%, reflecting a 0.88 
percentage point rise. Although restricted schemes 
continued to exhibit lower out-of-pocket costs, both 
open and restricted schemes experienced growth in 
member-paid costs, reinforcing concerns about the 
erosion of financial protection.
Prescribed Minimum Benefits and 
Disease Burden
The expenditure on Prescribed Minimum Benefits (PMB) 
increased substantially between 2023 and 2024, adding 
to the disease burden of schemes. The total PMB 
expenditure rose by 9.09%, from R124.36 billion to 
R135.66 billion, outpacing the overall benefit growth. As 
a result, PMBs accounted for 52.31% of total benefits 
paid in 2024, up from just under 52% in 2023. The PMB 
expenditure per beneficiary per month increased by 
8.49%, from R1 145.07 in 2023 to R1 242.27 in 2024, as 
depicted in Figure 2 below. The increase was particularly 
pronounced among beneficiaries aged 50 years and 
older, with the highest expenditure observed among 
those aged 85 years and above. These trends highlight 
the growing concentration of resources among a shrinking 
older cohort.
34
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
As far as finances are concerned, during 2024 medical 
schemes utilised their built-up reserves to fund their 
operations. Figure 3 illustrates that for every R100.00 
received in insurance revenue, an additional R3.07 of the 
reserves or investment income was required. It was 
utilised to fund the R96.18 paid in relevant healthcare 
expenditure, and R6.89 in directly attributable insurance 
service expenditure (DAE).
Insurance revenue per average beneficiary per month 
increased by 8.65% from R2 082.78 in 2023 to R2 262.90 
in 2024. This is significantly higher than the average CPI 
of 4.40% for the year. The higher increase in insurance 
revenue was necessary to compensate for the financial 
relief that was provided to members during the economic 
downturn post the COVID-19 pandemic. The relevant 
healthcare expenditure per average beneficiary per 
month increased by 9.03% from R1 996.12 in 2023 to R2 
176.35 in 2024. This exceeded the increase of 8.65% in 
the insurance revenue of the professional association of 
business process management, which exacerbated the 
current under-pricing of benefits even further. The higher 
claims increase is correlated to changes in tariffs and 
utilisation. Next, the focus will be on tariffs and how they 
are determined.
Tariffs
Historically, tariffs were determined through central 
negotiations between the South African Medical 
Association (SAMA) and the Board of Healthcare Funders 
(BHF). This practice ended in 2003 following the 
Competition Commission’s prohibition of collective 
bargaining under the Competition Act. Since then, tariffs 
are set bilaterally, which has raised concerns about 
transparency and consistency. The Health Market Inquiry 
(HMI) Final Report (September 2019) recommended, 
among other measures, the establishment of a multilateral 
negotiating framework for reference tariffs. It also 
recommended a national maximum tariff for Prescribed

Minimum Benefits (PMBs), and a shift towards Alternative 
Reimbursement Models. It furthermore highlighted the 
need for a supply-side regulator. On 14 February 2025, 
the Minister of Trade, Industry, and Competition published 
the Government Gazette No. 52111, inviting the public 
comment on the draft Interim Block Exemption for Tariffs 
Determination in the Healthcare Sector. The proposed 
exemption would allow for the collective determination 
of tariffs, coding standards, and quality metrics for both 
the PMBs and non-PMBs. All of this falls under a structured 
framework involving a Tariffs Governing Body and a 
Multilateral Negotiating Forum. The exemption is 
intended to apply for three years, subject to extension. 
The CMS supports the development of a multilateral 
pricing negotiation framework as a tool to enhance 
transparency and ensure the sustainability of medical 
schemes. There will now be a focus on utilisation in 
relation to market dynamics within the context of 
membership.
Utilisation
As far as utilisation is concerned, the CMS continues to 
monitor market dynamics, including an ageing 
membership base, benefit option proliferation, and rising 
healthcare costs. In 2024, medical scheme membership 
grew by only 0.56%, while the average age increased by 
0.29 years, contributing to higher utilisation rates. The 
CMS awaits the outcome of the National Department of 
Health’s (NDoH) review of the Low-Cost Benefit Options 
(LCBO) framework. It is also monitoring related legal 
proceedings, including the  appeal by the Board of Health 
Funders (BHF), following the High Court’s dismissal of its 
application in April 2025. A notable trend in 2024 was a 
significant increase in in-hospital cost per event, which 
seems to be driven by a supplier-induced demand. Many 
scheme rules provide for fully funded baskets of care for 
pre-authorised admissions, and an increased utilisation of 
services unrelated to the primary reason for admission 
has been observed. The CMS and the NDoH are 
collaborating on the development of a standardised 
benefit package and the review of PMBs. It is focused on 
establishing, costing, and implementing a Primary 
Healthcare (PHC) package of services as part of the PMBs. 
Efforts are also underway to align the CMS’ PHC package 
with the NDoH-NHI-PHC draft package. Updates on 
these initiatives are available on the CMS website under 
the Media Centre.
Figure 4 below shows the relationship between insurance 
revenue and the relevant healthcare expenditure paid 
over the past three years. 
Further releases in pent-up demand and utilisation 
increases due to an ageing population, resulted in an 
increased relevant healthcare expenditure ratio. Previous 
lower contribution increases to aid members during the 
economic downturn  also contributed to the increase. 
The year of 2024 saw a 0.35% increase in the relevant 
healthcare expenditure ratio to 96.18%. This is significantly 
higher than the pre-COVID 19 pandemic ratios of 90.23% 
and 90.58% in 2018 and 2019, respectively. It is anticipated 
that higher contribution increases will continue in the 
foreseeable future to address the misalignment between 
the benefits provided and their pricing. The CMS 
envisages repricing as an incremental process.

Open medical schemes incurred an insurance service 
deficit of R1.49 billion for the financial year ending 31 
December 2024. After accounting for investment income, 
the open scheme industry incurred a net surplus of R3.33 
billion. Restricted schemes similarly incurred an insurance 
service deficit of R5.99 billion for the year under review. 
However, after investment income was considered, the 
restricted scheme industry attained a net deficit of R0.20 
billion. It is clear that both the open and restricted scheme 
industries were under-priced on an insurance service 
result (or operational result) level for the 2024 year.
Figure 6 shows that the medical scheme industry is 
financially sound. The 2024 industry solvency ratio of 
40.87% exceeds the minimum required Regulation 29 
ratio of 25% (2023: 43.94%). Medical schemes price for a 
break-even result at an insurance result level. Pricing 
corrections and benefit adjustments are, therefore, 
necessary for future periods. Due to the industry’s healthy 
financial position, these corrections can be implemented 
incrementally.
Schemes under Close Monitoring
In order to sustain this financially healthy position, medical 
schemes  are required to maintain the statutory minimum 
solvency level of 25%. Medical schemes that fall short of 
this requirement are required to notify the CMS of the 
underlying causes of failure, and the corrective action is 
to be undertaken. Such schemes are then closely 
monitored by the CMS. Schemes that did not meet the 
required minimum solvency level of 25% account for 
2.52% of all medical scheme beneficiaries.

Open Schemes
As far as open schemes are concerned, a total of 4.93% 
of beneficiaries in open schemes were covered by 
Medihelp and Compcare Medical Scheme, which failed 
to meet the prescribed solvency level in 2024. Medihelp 
deliberately under-priced its benefits during the 
COVID-19 pandemic to provide relief to its members. 
The scheme experienced a 3.89% decrease in its 
insurance revenue per average beneficiary per month 
(pabpm) from 2021 to 2022 (refer to the 2022 Annexures). 
This was in contrast  to an average CPI of 6.9% during the 
same period. The scheme corrected their pricing for the 
2024 financial year and experienced an increase of 
13.79% in its insurance revenue pabpm, compared to the 
average CPI of 4.40%. The scheme submitted the 
required Business Plan in terms of Regulation 29, which 
was subsequently approved by the Registrar.
Schemes with higher demographic profiles are at 
particular risk of the so-called  ‘death spiral’. This is a 
scenario where adjustments to price appropriately for the 
profile of its members might result in the unaffordability 
of contributions. It might also result in  the subsequent 
loss of its younger members. All of this will  exacerbate 
the effect. Compcare Medical Scheme is a smaller medical 
scheme with a very poor demographic profile, and the 
scheme is therefore exposed to significant claims volatility 
risk. The scheme has restructured its benefits for the 2025 
financial year to address the scheme’s underlying 
membership risks. The Registrar has subsequently 
approved the scheme’s Regulation 29 Business Plan.
Restricted Schemes
When it comes to restricted schemes, no restricted 
medical schemes failed to meet the minimum required 
solvency level at the end of 2024 (Transmed Medical 
Fund attained 25.00% during 2024). 
Shift to Value-Based Healthcare
Globally, there is a clear shift towards Value-Based Health 
Care (VBHC), which prioritises improving healthcare 
quality and patient outcomes over merely increasing the 
volume of services. For medical schemes, this involves 
adopting payment models that reward providers for 
delivering care that demonstrably improves patient 
outcomes, such as outcomes-based or results-oriented 
care. However, transitioning to VBHC presents several 
challenges. The traditional Fee-for-Service (FFS) model 
incentivises high service volumes, which can result in 
over-treatment, higher costs, and a focus away from 
patient outcomes. Bundled payments, intended to 
control costs, require precise risk adjustment to avoid 
under-treatment, particularly for patients with complex 
medical conditions. Capitation models carry financial risk 
if actual care costs exceed fixed payments, potentially 
encouraging providers to restrict the necessary care.
Effective VBHC implementation also depends on robust 
data systems to measure performance accurately, while 
providers must adapt to new care models, which can be 
resource-intensive and operationally complex. In the 
South African context, developing a national framework, 
standards, and industry guidance for VBHC is critical. 
Such guidance would support medical schemes and 
providers in implementing outcomes-focused care. It 
would also ensure consistent and equitable practice, and 
use data-driven insights to improve quality, enhance 
health equity, and strengthen access to high-quality care 
across all populations.

Figure 8: Policy implications of the NHI with respect to the essential and minimum offered by schemes
National Health Insurance
The National Health Insurance (NHI) represents a 
transformative health financing system. It is designed to 
pool public resources into a single NHI Fund to purchase 
quality, affordable personal health services for all South 
Africans according to their needs, advancing Universal 
Health Coverage (UHC). The Fund will contract directly 
with public and private providers, allocating resources to 
hospitals, primary healthcare facilities, and emergency 
services. This will take place through global budgets, 
capitation, or case-based fees, with services determined 
by the Benefits Advisory Committee. Key components, 
including a single-payer system, standardised benefits, 
the integration of public and private providers, and a 
reduced role for medical schemes, are intended to 
broaden coverage. It is also intended to reduce inequities 
and ensure lower point-of-service costs. However, these 
reforms have significant implications for medical schemes. 
This includes a reduced operational role, potential 
membership and funding shifts, and the need to realign 
benefit packages to complement the NHI Basic Benefits 
Package. Schemes must also adopt governance, 
contracting, and claims processes to interface with the 
NHI Fund. This highlights the importance of proactive 
regulatory oversight and strategic adaptation to maintain 
financial sustainability and relevance in a transformed 
health system. Policy implications (also depicted in Figure 
8 below) include the need for legislation and regulatory 
frameworks that support scheme adaptation, protect 
consumer interests, and maintain social solidarity. At the 
same time, it must  ensure that private sector participation 
strengthens rather than undermines the objectives of 
UHC.
Due to this evolving landscape, the CMS, mandated 
under section 7(b) of the Medical Schemes Act (131 of 
1998), plays a pivotal regulatory and advisory role. The 
CMS ensures that schemes comply with governance, risk 
management, and consumer protection standards while 
facilitating their alignment with NHI objectives. This 
includes harmonising Prescribed Minimum Benefits with 
the NHI Basic Benefits Package and integrating primary 
healthcare coverage. It also includes advising on benefit 
structures, contribution models, and contracting 
arrangements, and participating in technical NHI 
committees. The CMS also supports the NDoH by 
providing technical expertise on scheme market 
structures, funding mechanisms, and operational 
performance. To mitigate the NHI-related challenges, the 
CMS has incorporated the NHI provisions into its Strategic 
Plan 2025–2030. This enables schemes to adapt while 
upholding social solidarity principles and protecting 
beneficiaries. Its strategy combines supportive oversight, 
evidence-based guidance, strengthened governance, 
and transparent communication to maintain stakeholder 
confidence. It ensures that medical schemes continue to 
operate sustainably and contribute effectively to the 
national health system while integrating seamlessly with 
NHI objectives. From the policy perspective, this 
underscores the critical need for coordinated regulation, 
clear guidance on complementary coverage, and 
mechanisms that incentivise private sector alignment with 
national health priorities.
Disease Management, Preventive Care and 
Emerging Gap
Another issue at stake for the CMS, is schemes’ approach 
to disease management, preventative care and emerging 
gaps. Disease management indicators reveal uneven 
progress between 2023 and 2024. While antiretroviral 
therapy (ART) coverage remained relatively stable, the 
gap between ART coverage and viral load suppression 
persisted across all age groups. The disparity was most

pronounced among beneficiaries aged 15–29 years, 
where the gap averaged 24%, compared to approximately 
10% among beneficiaries aged 65 years and older. 
Preventive care utilisation deteriorated further. Birth 
admissions declined by 3.93%, from 25.38 per 1 000 
beneficiaries in 2023 to 24.36 in 2024, with the sharpest 
declines observed in open schemes. Immunisation 
coverage declined dramatically following the cessation of 
targeted campaigns, with measles vaccination rates 
among children under 15 falling by 65.86% year-on-year. 
This brings us to the next section, which will focus on the 
economic outlook.
Economic Outlook
As far as the economic outlook is concerned, the picture 
looks bleak. Based on the updated gross domestic 
product (GDP) projections by the South African Reserve 
Bank (SARB) and the World Bank, we are experiencing a 
challenging economic recovery. This situation stems from 
analysing the economic recovery and key uncertainties in 
the CMS environment over the remaining five-year 
Strategic Planning cycle. Additionally, the CMS’ external 
environment is heavily influenced by extensive legal 
challenges and policy debates related to the government’s 
efforts to implement the National Health Insurance. This 
scenario also indicates reduced resources for the CMS to 
address its regulatory shortcomings, human resources, 
Information Technology (IT) systems, and other 
operational challenges. The combined impact of this 
scenario results in slow preparations towards regulatory 
effectiveness and efficiency and delayed delivery of the 
National Health Insurance (NHI) support programmes.
Key economic projections indicate modest GDP growth 
for South Africa, with a projected real growth of 1.2% in 
2025 as per the South African Reserve Bank annual 
forecasts. Slightly faster growth is expected for 2026 and 
2027 at 1.4% and 1.9% respectively. The real GDP growth 
is primarily driven by an easing of supply constraints, 
including improved energy security and improved 
logistics in critical sectors such as rail and ports. 
Additionally, the ageing population, increasing disease 
burden, and the escalating impacts of climate change 
present a growing need for the government to address 
the pressures that these factors place on public finances. 
Figure 9 highlights the recent low inflation trend, which 
has stabilised within the target range set by the central 
bank. For medical schemes, this provides a more 
predictable economic environment, supporting 
contribution affordability and facilitating long-term 
financial planning. While general inflation remains low, 
schemes must still manage healthcare-specific cost 
pressures that often exceed overall inflation. This 
influences benefit design, premium adjustments, and 
investment strategies to ensure sustainability and 
continued member protection.
Local Economic Outlook and its Impact on 
Medical Schemes
The outlook for medical schemes remains cautiously 
stable, but it is highly dependent on broader economic 
conditions. While moderating inflation and expected 
interest rate cuts may provide short-term relief, structural 
economic weaknesses constrain scheme sustainability, 
member affordability, and risk pooling capacity. Due to 
this, it is necessary to take a look at contribution 
affordability and household resilience.
Contribution Affordability and Household 
Resilience
Households remain financially strained despite slight 
gains in net wealth and lower debt-servicing costs. 
Persistent high living expenses, slow income growth, and 
household debt above 60% of disposable income 
continue to erode discretionary spending. As a result, 
medical scheme participation is increasingly vulnerable 
to lapses, benefit downgrades, and limited new 
membership, particularly among low- and middle-income 
groups.  Modest improvements in credit extension and

The effects of climate change, energy shortages, and
