impact on healthcare. Provinces like Limpopo, Northwest, 
Mpumalanga, KwaZulu-Natal, and Gauteng experienced 
the combined effects of these phenomena at some point. 
Legal and Regulatory Implementation 
These persistent environmental and economic challenges 
underscore the need for effective legal and regulatory 
implementation. During the 2025/26 financial year, the 
CMS continued to execute its regulatory mandate within 
a dynamic legal and policy environment characterised by 
ongoing health sector reforms. Legislative and regulatory 
developments continued to shape the regulation of 
medical schemes, requiring the CMS to strengthen its 
regulatory oversight while supporting national health 
policy objectives. The continued implementation of the 
NHI Act remained a significant development influencing 
the operating environment of the CMS. As the statutory 
regulator established under the Medical Schemes Act, 
the CMS executed important tasks. It actively monitored 
policy developments and assessed their implications for 
medical schemes, governance frameworks, benefit 
design, and the future role of private health financing. 
The CMS also continued to engage with the National 
Department of Health and other stakeholders to ensure 
that regulatory frameworks remain responsive to the 
evolving healthcare landscape.
During the year under review, the CMS continued to 
contribute to policy initiatives aimed at strengthening the 
sustainability, affordability, and accessibility of private 
healthcare funding. This included ongoing work relating 
to the review of the Prescribed Minimum Benefits (PMBs) 
framework and continued policy discussions regarding 
the Low-Cost Benefit Options (LCBOs). These initiatives 
seek to improve access to appropriate healthcare benefits 
while ensuring regulatory certainty and protecting the 
interests of medical scheme beneficiaries. The CMS also 
continued to consider the regulatory implications of the 
recommendations arising from the Competition 
Commission’s Health Market Inquiry (HMI). The 
recommendations continue to inform regulatory reforms 
aimed at improving governance, promoting market 
transparency, enhancing competition, and supporting 
value-based healthcare within the medical scheme’s 
environment.
In response to the evolving regulatory landscape, the 
CMS strengthened its supervisory and compliance 
functions through enhanced governance practices, risk-
based supervision, stakeholder engagement, and an 
ongoing review of regulatory frameworks. These initiatives 
support the CMS’ strategic objective of ensuring a 
sustainable, transparent, and well-regulated medical 
schemes industry while protecting the interests of 
beneficiaries. 
The implementation of Section 59 of the Medical Schemes 
Act remained a significant regulatory focus during the 
year under review. The CMS continued to oversee the 
implementation of corrective measures arising from the 
Section 59 investigation. The purpose was to ensure that 
medical schemes apply the provisions of the Act fairly, 
consistently, and in a manner that protects the rights of 
beneficiaries and healthcare providers. The CMS 
monitored compliance with agreed governance and 
administrative reforms by medical schemes. It also 
promoted greater transparency in claims administration 
and stakeholder engagement and it continued to 
strengthen regulatory oversight to improve accountability 
and public confidence in the medical schemes industry.
The CMS remains committed to working collaboratively 
with the National Department of Health, other regulators, 
medical schemes, healthcare providers, and industry 
stakeholders. The purpose is to support effective 
implementation of legislative reforms and to ensure that 
the regulatory framework continues to promote sound 
governance, financial sustainability, and equitable access 
to quality healthcare. In light of the above, it is also 
necessary to look at the CMS in the context of the G20 
health priorities and the broader implications that impact 
the health industry.
G20 Health Priorities and Implications for 
the CMS
South Africa’s leadership of the G20 Health Working 
Group in 2025 reinforced the global commitment to 
advancing Universal Health Coverage (UHC), 
strengthening health system resilience, and improving 
equitable access to quality healthcare. The G20 Health 
Ministerial Statement emphasised the importance of 
reducing health inequalities, improving access to essential 
medicines, and promoting sustainable healthcare 
financing.
These priorities are closely aligned with the mandate of 
the CMS. Through its regulatory oversight of the medical 
schemes industry, the CMS contributes to protecting 
beneficiaries. It also promotes equitable access to 
healthcare, supports healthcare affordability, and 
strengthens governance within the private health 
financing sector.
The G20 outcomes further support South Africa’s ongoing 
health sector reforms, including the implementation of 
the NHI, by highlighting the importance of resilient, 
inclusive, and sustainable health financing systems. The 
CMS remains committed to fostering a well-regulated 
medical schemes environment that advances consumer 
protection, financial sustainability, and equitable access 
to quality healthcare in support of national and global 
health objectives. However, the CMS also must contend 
with geopolitical tensions, which impact the global 
economy and the health industry as such.
41
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Geopolitical Tensions
Import Dependency
The first challenge that the CMS has to face is the issue of 
import dependency. South Africa remains reliant on 
imported pharmaceuticals, medical devices, diagnostic 
equipment, and specialised healthcare consumables. 
Escalating geopolitical tensions between Iran and the 
United States have the potential to disrupt global trade 
routes and shipping networks. This is a particular threat if 
instability affects the Strait of Hormuz, one of the world’s 
most critical maritime trade routes for oil and international 
shipping. Such disruptions can increase shipping times, 
freight costs, and supply chain uncertainty, resulting in 
longer procurement lead times and a reduced availability 
of essential medical supplies. Healthcare providers may 
also face delays in obtaining critical medicines, laboratory 
reagents, surgical equipment, and other products 
required for uninterrupted patient care.
Increased Costs
Another challenging issue is an increase in costs across 
various sectors. Geopolitical instability can contribute to 
higher global energy prices, increased freight and 
logistics costs, and broader inflationary pressures. 
Disruptions to oil exports through the Strait of Hormuz 
may result in higher fuel prices, increasing the cost of 
manufacturing, transporting, and importing medical 
products and healthcare consumables. Rising procurement 
costs place additional pressure on healthcare budgets 
and may contribute to higher medical scheme premiums 
and increased out-of-pocket healthcare costs for patients. 
Inflationary pressures may also affect operational 
expenses, including utilities, transport, maintenance, and 
outsourced services, reducing the purchasing power of 
healthcare institutions.
Service Delivery Environment
The service delivery environment is also prone to 
challenges. Supply chain disruptions and inflationary 
pressures may adversely affect the continuity and 
efficiency of healthcare service delivery. Delays in the 
availability of essential medicines, medical devices, and 
consumables could impact treatment schedules, elective 
procedures, and overall patient care. To mitigate these 
risks, the organisation continues to strengthen supply 
chain resilience through supplier diversification, strategic 
inventory management, an ongoing monitoring of 
geopolitical developments, and business continuity 
planning. These measures support the continued delivery 
of safe, reliable, and quality healthcare services despite 
an increasingly uncertain global operating environment.
Economic Outlook
The general economic outlook remains bleak due to 
various challenges. Persistent geopolitical tensions may 
weaken the global economic growth, reduce investor 
confidence, and contribute to exchange rate volatility. 
For South Africa, these developments could result in 
sustained inflation, higher import costs, and slower 
economic growth, placing additional pressure on the 
healthcare sector. Continued uncertainty may affect both 
public healthcare funding and private sector affordability, 
reinforcing the need for prudent financial management, 
operational efficiency, and proactive risk mitigation.
Life Expectancy and Health Expenditure in 
South Africa
Life expectancy and health expenditure are further issues 
of importance. South Africa’s health outcomes continued 
to improve modestly during 2024, despite persistent 
economic and healthcare system challenges. According 
to Macro-trends, life expectancy at birth increased from 
64.88 years in 2023 to 65.10 years in 2024. This reflects 
ongoing improvements in access to healthcare services, 
HIV/AIDS treatment programmes, maternal and child 
healthcare, and the management of communicable and 
non-communicable diseases.
South Africa continues to allocate a relatively high 
proportion of its GDP to healthcare when compared  to 
many upper-middle-income countries. However, higher 
levels of health expenditure have not translated into life 
expectancy outcomes comparable with those of several 
peer economies. This reflects the influence of broader 
structural factors, including socioeconomic inequality, 
disparities in access to quality healthcare, the dual public-
private healthcare system, the burden of disease, and 
inefficiencies within the health system.
For the medical schemes industry, these trends highlight 
the importance of improving healthcare efficiency, 
promoting preventive care, strengthening access to 
quality healthcare services, and ensuring the sustainability 
of healthcare financing. Continued collaboration between 
regulators, healthcare providers, medical schemes, and 
policymakers remains essential to improving health 
outcomes while maintaining affordable and equitable 
access to healthcare.
42
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
As far as the organisational environment is concerned, 
under the Public Finance Management Act (PFMA), 
public entities must submit quarterly reports to their 
relevant executive authority. Therefore, in line with the 
provisions of the PFMA, during the period under review, 
the CMS submitted detailed quarterly reports for both 
the financial and non-financial performance. This 
measured progress against the targets and objectives set 
out in the Strategic Plans and Annual Performance Plans. 
These were submitted to the Executive Authority and the 
Department of Planning, Monitoring and Evaluation 
(DPME), respectively, and no concerns were raised. All 
the quarterly financial and non-financial performance 
reports showed excellent performance by the CMS under 
the direction and guidance of the Council. However, 
during the period under review, some targets were not 
achieved due to technological challenges and loss of 
institutional knowledge because of the resignations and 
retirements of critical skills. 
Key Policy Developments and Legislative 
Changes
The focus will now be on key policy developments and 
legislative changes. In Chapter 2 of the Constitution of 
the Republic of South Africa, the Bill of Rights underscores 
the obligation of the State to enact reasonable legislative 
and other measures to ensure accessible healthcare 
services for all citizens. Section 27 guarantees access to 
healthcare services, food, water, and social security. In 
contrast, Section 24 safeguards the right to an environment 
free from harm to the health or well-being of South 
Africans. 
The NDoH launched the National Strategic Plan for the 
Prevention and Control of Non-Communicable Diseases, 
2022–2027, emphasising integrated, person-centred 
approaches to address non-communicable diseases and 
mental health. It focused on improving care by medical 
schemes for chronic conditions like hypertension and 
diabetes. The revised National Strategic Plan (NSP) 
crafted by the South African National AIDS Council 
(SANAC) reflects a strategic realignment of interventions 
pertaining to HIV. This updated plan delineates ambitious 
strategic objectives aimed at diminishing obstacles to 
accessing healthcare and social services, particularly for 
individuals living with HIV, tuberculosis (TB), and sexually 
transmitted infections (STIs). 
Historically, these populations have encountered 
challenges in accessing and benefiting from treatment 
and care services. The NSP for HIV, TB, and STIs 2023–
2028 outlines multi-sectoral objectives and priority 
interventions to strengthen South Africa’s response to 
these epidemics. The CMS can support these goals by 
ensuring that medical schemes provide adequate 
coverage for HIV, TB, and STI-related services. This will 
promote access to preventative care, treatment, and 
adherence programmes, and align scheme benefits and 
management practices with national health priorities to 
enhance population health outcomes. 
The NSP 2023–2028 prioritises reducing inequalities and 
improving access to essential services for all affected 
individuals. It highlights the interconnectedness of various 
health and social issues, including mental health, sexual 
and gender-based violence (SGBV), human rights 
violations, and viral hepatitis. By addressing these 
concerns comprehensively, the NSP 2023–2028 
represents a holistic approach to promoting health equity 
and ensuring access to essential services for all individuals 
affected by these health challenges. The commitment of 
the South African Government to ensuring universal 
access to healthcare services has been steadfast since 
1994. It has  a focus on establishing a healthcare system 
that promotes preventive measures alongside treatment. 
This proactive approach addresses health concerns 
before they escalate, ensuring timely intervention when 
individuals’ health is compromised. 
To bolster these efforts, the government has outlined key 
priorities in the Medium-Term Strategic Framework 
(MTSF), aligning them with the broader goals of the 
National Development Plan 2030. This strategic 
framework delineates a comprehensive vision for 
expanding access to quality healthcare services. This 
vision also aims at reducing health disparities, enhancing 
healthcare infrastructure and resources, strengthening 
workforce capacity, and prioritising preventive healthcare 
measures. These initiatives are critical to realising the NHI 
vision, which aims to achieve universal healthcare 
coverage by eliminating financial barriers to healthcare 
access for all South Africans.
The NDoH Strategic Plan 2025–2030 outlines South 
Africa’s roadmap to achieving UHC through the NHI, 
emphasising equitable access, quality care, and financial 
protection. This plan is supported by various health sector 
policies and strategies that guide implementation over 
the five-year planning period. Key national and 
international frameworks further inform these efforts, 
including the National Development Plan: Vision 2030. 
The latter provides long-term strategic priorities for 
sustainable development, and the Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs), which set global targets for 
health, equity, and well-being. The Medium-Term 
Strategic Framework 2024–2029 outlines medium-term 
government priorities and strategies to transform the 
health sector, while the National Health Insurance Act 
provides the legal foundation for implementing the NHI. 
Additionally, the Presidential Health Compact reflects a 
high-level commitment between the government and 
health stakeholders to accelerate reforms and enhance 
accountability in the health system.
The CMS, through a consultation process with the 
industry, compiled the LCBO Report. The purpose was to

Policy Mandates
The CMS operates under various policy mandates that 
guide its strategic direction and operational priorities. 
Illustrated below are the most relevant frameworks and 
policies that were implemented to influence the mandate 
of the CMS.
Policy Description CMS’ Role
United Nations Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs)
The aim of SDG 3 is to  ‘ensure healthy lives and promote well-being 
for all at all ages’. Good health is essential for individuals to reach 
their full potential, as both physical health and mental well-being 
significantly affect the quality of life. 
 In South Africa, the health and well-being of the population are 
crucial for economic performance and social development. Achieving 
SDG 3 is directly related to improving health services, as accessible 
and quality healthcare is fundamental to fostering overall health and 
well-being.
South Africa subscribes to the United 
Nations SDGs. The CMS, a public 
entity, supports this imperative.
National Development Plan 
Vision 2030 (NDP)
The NDP seeks to eradicate poverty and decrease inequality by 
2030. It asserts that South Africa can achieve these objectives by 
harnessing the energies of its people. This will also be achieved 
by developing an inclusive economy, strengthening capabilities, 
improving the capacity of the State, and fostering leadership and 
partnerships across society.
The NDP outlines a strategic vision for South Africa’s development, 
including nine key priorities related to health: 
1. Raise the life expectancy of South Africans to at least 70 years 
by 2030
2. Progressively improve TB prevention and cure
3. Reduce maternal, infant, and child mortality
4. Significantly reduce the prevalence of non-communicable 
diseases
5. Reduce injury, accidents, and violence by 50 %   from 2010 levels
6. Complete health system reforms
7. Primary healthcare teams provide care to families and 
communities
8. Universal health coverage, and
9. Fill posts with skilled, suitably qualified, committed, and 
competent individuals.
Additionally, the NDP Vision 2030 outlines nine priority areas that 
emphasise the essential interventions needed to create a more 
effective health system:
1. Address the social determinants that affect health and diseases
2. Strengthen the health system
3. Improve health information systems
4. Prevent and reduce the disease burden and promote health
5. Finance universal healthcare coverage
6. Improve human resources in the health sector
7. Review management positions and appointments and 
strengthen accountability mechanisms, and
8. Improve quality by using evidence
9. Cultivate meaningful public–private partnerships.
The CMS, as a public entity, 
discharges its legislated mandate in 
a manner that is consistent with the 
national policy including the NDP 
Vision 2030.
44
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Policy Description CMS’ Role
The Charter for South Africa’s 
Public and Private Health 
Sectors, 2006 (Health Charter)
The Health Charter was initiated in support of the NHA and provides 
for the public and private health sectors to constructively engage 
in discussions to create an improved healthcare delivery system for 
South Africa. 
Such a system targets the coherent, efficient, cost-effective, quality-
driven, and optimised use of all sector resources to benefit all citizens.
The CMS supports the NHA by 
regulating private healthcare 
insurance providers. To this end, it 
therefore contributes to the progress 
of the Health Charter.
National Health Insurance (NHI) The NHI is South Africa’s strategy to achieve universal health 
coverage. The NHI is a centralised, national insurance fund through 
which the government will source healthcare services from healthcare 
providers in both public and private sectors.
The NHI pursues the Constitutional right of equal access to quality 
and affordable healthcare.
The CMS regulates the activities of 
medical schemes and other affected 
key stakeholders in the NHI.
The Second Presidential 
Health Compact 2024–2029 
(Presidential Health Compact)
The Second Presidential Health Summit, 2023 with the theme 
‘Accelerating Health System Strengthening and NHI Implementation’, 
reinforced the nine-pillar plan finalised at the Presidential Health 
Compact. These pillars are:
1. Augment Human Resources for Operational Health Plan 
2. Better management of supply chain equipment and machinery 
can ensure improved access to essential medicines, vaccines, 
and medical products. 
3. Execute the infrastructure plan to ensure adequate, appropriately 
distributed, well-maintained health facilities. 
4. Engage the private sector in improving health services’ access, 
coverage, and quality.
5. Improve health services’ quality, safety, and quantity, focusing on 
primary health care.
6. Improve the efficiency of public sector financial management 
systems and processes.
7. Strengthen governance and leadership to improve oversight, 
accountability, and health system performance at all levels.
8. Engage and empower the community to ensure adequate and 
appropriate community-based care, and 
9. Develop an information system to guide the health system’s 
policies, strategies, and investments. 
The main thrust of the Presidential Health Compact is to ensure that 
the health system can successfully execute the obligations of the NHI 
framework. Key common themes emphasise the need for expanded 
health workforce development and deployment. This will address 
inequities, strengthen health systems,   improve quality , eliminate 
corruption, maladministration and incompetence, and strengthen 
governance at national, provincial, district, ward, and facility levels. 
The CMS participates in the Steering Committee. In 2025, the 
Registrar and CEO was appointed as the Chair of Pillar 5 of the 2nd  
Presidential Health Compact. This pillar focuses on improving the 
quality, safety, and quantity of health services, with a strong emphasis 
on strengthening primary health care (PHC) in South Africa.
The CMS regulates the activities of 
medical schemes, key stakeholders in 
the NHI, and the Presidential Health 
Compact.
Recommendations of the Health 
Market Inquiry, 2019 (HMI 
Recommendations)
The Competition Commission’s HMI investigated the dynamics in the 
private healthcare market, identifying barriers to competition, and 
examining the factors that influence private healthcare costs.
In March 2025, during a briefing to the Portfolio Committee on 
Health, the CMS provided an update on the implementation plan 
for the Section 59 report. It also provided an update on progress 
in implementing the recommendations of the Health Market Inquiry 
(HMI).
The regulatory functions of the CMS 
are integral to supporting the HMI 
Recommendations insofar as these 
affect medical schemes.
Table 1: Policy Mandates and the CMS’ Role
45
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Due to all these challenges, the CMS is implementing 
various policy reforms. The central aim of the policy 
reforms provided for in the MSA is to enhance the risk 
pooling potential of medical schemes and strengthen 
important regulatory and oversight mechanisms by 
implementing the following reforms: 
• A preferred health insurance vehicle, which requires 
that any person doing the business of a medical scheme 
operate in terms of a single legislative framework. 
• Open enrolment, which removed the discriminatory 
practice of medical schemes to select only good-risk  
beneficiaries for membership (risk selection). 
• Mandatory minimum benefits, which removed the 
ability of schemes to discriminate against older and   
sicker members through the selective non-provision 
of key benefits. 
• Waiting periods and late joiner penalties: to eliminate 
any significant application of penalties regarding 
member movement between medical schemes and 
options while substantially removing the opportunities 
for anti-selection where a member joins only when sick 
and then leaves or only joins for the first time later in 
life. 
• Improved governance, which removed the historical  
conflicts of interest embedded in the oversight of 
medical schemes. 
• Regulation of intermediaries, which implemented 
accreditation and more stringent regulatory oversight 
of medical scheme brokers, administrators, and 
managed care organisations. 
• Member protection, which includes the complaints 
resolution mechanisms at the scheme level and provides 
members access to the complaints’ resolution 
mechanisms at the Registrar’s office and appeals 
processes. These reforms remain relevant to this day. 
The original intentions behind the  introduction of 
the above measures were to ensure that all health 
funders operate on a level playing field. It should 
maximise the advantages and minimise the 
disadvantages of competing in a highly commer c ialised 
multi-fund health industry. However, many facets of 
the funding and provision of private health services 
are still not adequately regulated, resulting in systemic 
shortfalls in coverage, affecting the quality of coverage, 
cost containment, and the impact on the public health 
system. 
Information & Communications Technology
Digital Transformation
Digital transformation also necessitates policy and 
regulation reforms. The rapid growth of telemedicine, 
artificial intelligence (AI), and digital health tools is 
reshaping the healthcare landscape. As medical schemes 
invest in digital infrastructure to enhance member 
engagement and improve operational efficiencies, the 
CMS faces significant challenges and opportunities to 
regulate these aspects. Effective regulation in the digital 
health space requires collaboration among various 
stakeholders, including healthcare providers, technology 
developers, and medical schemes. The CMS plans to 
engage with these groups and other material stakeholders. 
The purpose is to understand the challenges and 
opportunities posed by digital transformation and to 
develop regulations that support efficiencies in the 
healthcare ecosystem.
The CMS has embarked on a digital transformation 
journey. Several operational digital technological projects 
have been delivered to ensure that the CMS is able to 
function and continue as an organisation through its daily 
operations. Amidst the backdrop of challenges, the CMS 
is making headway in our digitisation journey. The annual 
returns from medical schemes and the annual medical 
industry report have been delivered by using big data 
platforms. This was to ensure that key mandate items of 
the CMS have been met for the industry and our members. 
We have also implemented the first endorsed artificial 
intelligence (AI) tool governed by the first AI approved 
policy for the CMS as an organisation. The Technology 
department of the CMS has also met all KPI’s for the 
organisation overall. This occurred against the backdrop 
of limited budgets, constrained resources, and skills 
limitations due to new technology embedment into the 
CMS’ technological environments across all spheres. Our 
cybersecurity tools and controls remain resilient to ‘future-
proof’ the CMS . Twelve technology policies have been 
approved, and our approved Technology Governance 
framework is also progressing steadily in its implementation 
to ensure that we deliver on our new technologies in the 
right way.
Financial Management 
Due to all these challenges, financial management also 
requires adjustments with regard to policy and regulation. 
In the area of financial management, the CMS has 
matured significantly as evidenced by the unqualified 
audit reports issued by the Auditor-General of South 
Africa over the years. There are, however, areas which 
require much improvement. The main area of focus will 
be strengthening the supply chain management aspect. 
To this end, a proposal has been made to have a dedicated 
supply chain management unit which is fully capacitated 
in line with developing trends.  
Management must ensure that the financial management 
that  controls the CMS internally is strengthened and that 
policies are reviewed and applied consistently. An area 
that requires further attention is that of consequence 
management in cases where there has been non-
compliance with policies and regulations. The CMS has in 
place relevant governance structures to oversee the 
financial environment of the entity. The Audit and Risk 
Committee operate under an approved Charter and 
oversees the work of the internal auditors.   
POLICY REFORMS IMPLEMENTATION

46
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
In the last five years, the CMS has encountered significant 
financial challenges and other resource constraints. The 
delayed approval of the proposed levy increase, coupled 
with the capping of the proposed increase to the 
Consumer Price Index (CPI), has had a detrimental effect 
on the organisation’s cash flow. It has also hampered  its 
ability to meet all its responsibilities as a statutory 
regulator. The CMS has employed operational strategies 
to bolster its financial sustainability. However, it is evident 
that a more strategic approach is essential to ensure the 
organisation’s long-term financial stability. As a result, the 
CMS will shift its focus from solely managing its budget to 
evaluating and enhancing its funding model with a long-
term approach.
Additionally, the CMS’ audit outcome has displayed 
improvement over the past five years, reflecting enhanced 
performance, compliance, and financial management. 
The objective for the upcoming strategic period is to 
strive for a clean audit outcome, therefore signifying a 
credible and well-governed regulator.
Human Resources
Human resources are also impacted by challenges and, 
as a result, policies need to be revised and updated. A 
high-level review of the remuneration philosophy and 
employment value proposition was undertaken by an 
external service provider. Due to budget constraints, the 
CMS is currently unable to fully sustain the employee 
benefits outlined in the policy. Consequently, 
recommendations were made on specific policy elements 
for the Council’s consideration, with a view to consult staff 
and ensure compliance with applicable employment 
legislation. In ensuring compliance with legislation 
governing employment during the period under review, 
the human resources sub-programme reviewed policies 
and updated policies accordingly. The Human Resources 
Unit completed the data collection and analysis for the 
project assessing the effectiveness of the organisational 
structure during the reporting period
Member Protection through Resolution of 
Complaints and Queries 
Another area of importance is member protection through 
the resolution of complaints and queries. Section 7(a) of 
the Medical Schemes Act mandates the CMS to always 
protect the interests of medical scheme beneficiaries. 
One of the ways in which the CMS carries out this mandate 
is through the investigation and resolution of complaints. 
Over the past five years, the volume of incoming 
complaints has been following a cyclical pattern. 
The 2022/23 financial year represented the peak, followed 
by a decrease for two consecutive years (2023/24 and 
2024/25). A new pattern is now emerging, with a sustained 
increase over the 2025/26 financial year. The period 
between 2020/21 and 2024/25 yielded positive outcomes 
for member protection, with significant clearance of the 
backlog, and an increase in resolution rates of 86% during 
the past years of 2023/24 and 2024/25 respectively. 
Figure 10 below is an overview of complaints received 
and resolved over the past 5 years, specifically highlighting 
the number of complaints received versus the number of 
complaints resolved. On average, the number of 
complaints resolved over the years exceeded the number 
that was received.

Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment 
(B-BBEE) Initiatives 
Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment (B-BBEE) 
initiatives are an important aspect of transformation in 
the industry. The government has an obligation to harness 
national resources towards the resolution of the historical 
injustice of racial, gender, and class exclusion in all 
spheres of life.  In the realm of economic life, this implies 
the need to transform the patterns of asset ownership. It 
must be done in a manner that reinforces the national 
objective of building a South African society that truly 
belongs to all who live in it.  The government has 
developed various policy instruments, such as the 
Preferential Procurement Regulations of 2017 (PPR) 
issued in terms of the Preferential Procurement Policy 
Framework Act 5 of 2000 (PPPFA). The  objective of the 
PPPFA is to transform the structure of the South African 
economy in a manner that advances previously 
disadvantaged persons or categories of persons, ensuring 
high levels of decent employment and the demographic 
transformation of industrial assets.
The CMS is committed to mainstreaming enterprise 
growth, empowerment, and equity in the economy for 
designated groups through the National Treasury 
requirements and the CMS Supply Chain Management 
(SCM) Policy. In this way, the CMS will continue to leverage 
its procurement spend to advance historically 
disadvantaged persons. It will be done by giving 
preference to designated groups (aligned to the PPPFA), 
exempt micro enterprises (EMEs), and qualifying small 
enterprises (QSEs).  
Other B-BBEE initiatives
Other B-BBEE initiatives were implemented to support 
the achievement of the CMS’ transformation goals. The 
CMS applied the B-BBEE Codes of Good Practice and 
the 80/20 preference point system for the procurement of 
goods and services valued from R2 001 up to R50 million, 
and the 90/10 preference point system for transactions 
exceeding R50 million, where applicable.
More specific measures taken by the CMS include the 
following:
• Requesting and ensuring that each bidder submits an 
affidavit and B-BBEE credentials when responding to 
the invitation for bids.
• Using the quote evaluation system to allocate points  
for specific goals in line with the Preferential Procurement 
Regulations 2022.
Status of the CMS’ Interventions Relating to 
Women and Youth
The status of the CMS’ interventions relating to women 
and youth remains an important area of focus. The CMS 
contributes to the strategic priorities of the 7 th 
Administration relating to inclusive economic growth and 
the empowerment of designated groups.
The empowerment and equitable representation of 
designated groups remain key strategic priorities for the 
CMS. In this regard, the CMS seeks to promote equitable 
representation of suitably qualified persons from 
designated groups across all occupational levels of the 
workforce.
Over the past five years, women represented an average 
of 46% of the CMS’ workforce, while youth represented 
an average of 22%, as indicated in the tables below. The 
CMS will continue to promote the representation and 
participation of women and youth across the organisation.

The following section pertains to the Strategic Outcomes in relation to the Strategic Plan. The CMS has developed the 
following Strategic Outcomes for the 2025–2030 Strategic Plan aligned with the 2024–29 Medium-Term Strategic 
Framework (MTSF):
Outcome 1 Regulating entities in compliance with the National Policy, the Medical Schemes Act, and other legislation
Progress Review 
Statement
Regarding the regulation of entities in compliance with National Policy, the MSA and other legislation, the CMS 
has developed the following Strategic Outcomes for the 2025–2030 Strategic Plan. It is aligned with the 2024–29 
Medium-Term Strategic Framework (MTSF):
The CMS will register and accredit all entities involved in the medical scheme business. It will also review and 
approve scheme rules, manage complaints, conduct compliance inspections, and monitor the financial soundness 
of these entities. The purpose is to ensure effective regulation in accordance with the Medical Schemes Act, National 
Policy, and other relevant legislation.
This is all aimed at providing scheme members with the protection that they need and improving access to affordable 
and quality healthcare. The CMS demonstrated strong regulatory performance during the reporting period, with 
most output indicators either meeting or exceeding planned targets. This reflects sustained institutional capability 
in processing regulatory submissions, strengthening compliance oversight, and improving operational efficiencies 
within the regulatory function.
In the 2025/26 financial period the CMS received 4 654 broker and broker organisation applications for accreditation.  
From the 4 654 applications received, 4 350 applications were accredited within 30 working days of receipt of 
complete information. The CMS continued to verify the academic qualifications of individuals applying to be 
accredited as brokers. The qualifications of 782 individuals were verified independently during the period under 
review. The total number of accredited brokers and broker organisations as of 31 March 2026  were 7 793 and 2 
182, respectively.
During the 2025/26 year, the CMS completed the evaluation of  four limited services administrator, eight 
administrator, and four self-administered schemes’ administration accreditation/compliance certificate applications. 
It also completed the evaluation of 13 managed care organisations and one scheme providing its own managed 
care services to its members. In all these cases the CMS also evaluated the managed care accreditation/compliance 
certificate applications. As of 31 March 2026, there were 14 accredited administrators; 9 limited services 
administrators; 13 medical schemes with administration compliance certificates; 35 accredited managed care 
organisations and 7 medical schemes with managed care compliance certificates. Performance under Output 18 
(Medical scheme rules comply with the Medical Schemes Act) was largely achieved.
Output Indicator 18.1 (percentage of rule amendments processed within 30 working days) exceeded the planned 
target of 80%, achieving 88.9% (48/54). This represents a +8.9% positive deviation. This is attributable to improved 
workflow management, enhanced internal performance monitoring, and the implementation of structured processes 
that ensure faster turnaround times once complete submissions are received. The integration of technical and 
administrative functions further reduced processing delays and improved responsiveness to stakeholders.
Output Indicator 18.2 (percentage of annual rule amendments processed before 31 December) achieved 100% 
(69/69) against a target of 95%, reflecting a 5% positive deviation. This outcome demonstrates high levels of 
regulatory efficiency and discipline, supported by early stakeholder engagement, strengthened internal planning, 
and proactive follow-up mechanisms. These measures ensured that submissions were complete and compliant at 
the point of review, allowing for uninterrupted processing and timely approvals of benefit and contribution changes. 
This  is critical for scheme sustainability and member protection. 
Output Indicator 18.3 (the publication of Contributions and Benefits Changes Guidance Notes) remained stable, 
with the annual target achieved. This continues to support regulatory certainty and consistency in how medical 
schemes design contribution increases and benefit changes, reinforcing compliance with legislative and policy 
requirements.
Output Indicator 18.4 (the review and publication of Model Rules) was not achieved, with no outputs recorded 
against the planned target. The delay is attributable to the need for additional legal review and quality assurance 
processes. The purpose is to ensure that the Model Rules are fully compliant with the Medical Schemes Act and 
are legally sound and implementable. While this resulted in a short-term performance gap, it reflects a deliberate 
prioritisation of regulatory integrity over speed, given the foundational importance of Model Rules in guiding 
scheme governance and member relations.
Impact statement To be an agile and transformative regulator to promote affordable and accessible healthcare cover 
towards universal health coverage.
Progress Review Statement To be an agile and transformative regulator to promote affordable and accessible healthcare cover 
towards universal health coverage.

50
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Outcome 1 Regulating entities in compliance with the National Policy, the Medical Schemes Act, and other legislation
Progress Review 
Statement
To address this under-performance, the organisation will strengthen delivery through enhanced project planning, 
earlier initiation of the review process, and the allocation of dedicated legal and technical resources. In addition, 
improved milestone tracking and integrated quality assurance processes will be implemented to minimise delays 
and ensure timely publication during the next reporting cycle.
In order to ensure the continued financial soundness of the medical schemes industry, the Regulation 29 Business 
Plan Compcare Medical Scheme was analysed and approved. 
Limited reliance could be placed on the completeness of Sizwe Hosmed Medical Scheme’s claims for decision-
making purposes, which resulted in a rapidly declining solvency. Therefore, the Council made the decision to place 
the scheme under curatorship.
Based on unaudited data, only three schemes fell below the minimum Regulation 29 solvency level at the end of 
31 December 2025:
• Government Employees Medical Scheme (GEMS) at 24.70%. The scheme has submitted a Regulation 29   
 business plan during 2026.
• Sizwe Hosmed Medical Fund at 14.07%. The Scheme was placed under curatorship.
• Compcare Medical Scheme at 21.59%. The Scheme has submitted an updated Regulation 29 business plan  
 during 2026. The phase-in solvency level for the 2026 year was subsequently approved.
• Medihelp achieved the minimum required level and ended the year with a (unaudited) solvency of 26.92%.
During the 2025/26 financial period the CMS conducted routine inspections to monitor compliance in four medical 
schemes namely Fishmed, MBMed, Keyhealth and BPMAS. The purpose was to ensure effective regulation in 
accordance with the Medical Schemes Act, National Policy, and other relevant legislation. The selection of the 
schemes is made using a risk-based approach. Positive feedback was received from the schemes. It related to  the 
value added by the CMS through its recommendations and advice to the schemes. The advice pertained to how 
they should ensure compliance with the MSA and other relevant legislations and most importantly on acting in the 
best interest of the scheme beneficiaries.
In addition to the routine inspections conducted to monitor compliance with the Act, the CMS also initiated 
investigations in terms of section 44(4)(a) triggered by the allegations against two schemes. These were Bonitas 
and Makoti medical schemes. The Makoti medical scheme investigation is being conducted in-house by the CMS.
The CMS continues to monitor the Insurers and Financial Service Providers (FSPs) exempted from section 20(1) of 
the MSA compliance with the Demarcation Exemption Framework and its Regulations. The purpose is to protect the 
interest of the policy holders of such products.
The CMS also attended about 44 AGMs of the schemes on an observatory level to ensure that the AGMs are 
conducted in a fair and transparent manner. Governance interventions and enforcement actions were also taken in 
the form of directives issued to the regulated entities and Registrar enquiries in terms of the MSA.
Outcome 2 To promote access to quality, cost-effective, and affordable private healthcare financing and related private 
healthcare
Progress Review 
Statement
The CMS reports the utilisation of health services and monitors the prevalence of chronic conditions in the medical 
schemes’ population. Other monitoring processes include the continued collection of the Scheme Risk Measurement 
(SRM) data to measure and report on the risk profiles of medical schemes and benefit options. This allows schemes 
to improve its understanding of  the impact of age and chronic diseases on the beneficiaries covered by medical 
schemes.
The CMS also advanced the review of the Prescribed Minimum Benefits (PMBs) by progressing the development 
of a comprehensive Primary Healthcare (PHC) package. The proposed package includes tuberculosis (TB) and 
HIV treatment in line with public sector treatment protocols, together with preventive healthcare services such as 
screening and vaccination programmes, mental healthcare, basic dental services, and rehabilitative services. These 
reforms support improved financial protection, expanded access to essential healthcare services, reduced out-of-
pocket expenditure, and contributed to improved national health outcomes.
To improve health literacy and empower beneficiaries, the CMS published 12 CMScripts during the reporting 
period. The publications provided evidence-based information on priority health conditions and healthcare 
benefits,. It included polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), pregnancy benefits, autism, neurogenic bladder disorder, 
cerebral palsy, sepsis, pulmonary diseases, anaemia, depression, sexual violence, colon cancer, and emergency 
care. The CMScripts promoted greater awareness of healthcare conditions, available medical scheme benefits and 
the appropriate utilisation of healthcare services. This enables beneficiaries to make informed healthcare decisions 
and supporting access to quality, cost-effective, and affordable private healthcare.
51
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Outcome 3 To encourage risk pooling and innovation for the effective functioning of the private healthcare financing 
sector
Progress Review 
Statement
The CMS continued to promote equitable access to affordable, quality healthcare by advancing evidence-based 
reforms to strengthen benefit design, improve risk pooling, and enhance the sustainability of medical schemes. 
During the period under review, the CMS published the annual Contributions Guidance Note. The purpose was 
to support affordability and the Multilateral Negotiating Forum (MLNF), and strengthen collaboration with the 
National Department of Health and the Competition Commission to improve regulatory coherence and healthcare 
affordability.
An annual assessment of medical schemes with fewer than 6 000 principal members found that 30 small risk pool 
schemes remained in operation. This represents 42% of registered medical schemes but only 2.11% of industry 
beneficiaries. While these schemes maintained solvency levels above the statutory minimum, the assessment found 
that they experienced higher claims volatility (16.73% compared to 9.45% for larger schemes). They also had older 
and higher-risk membership profiles, and higher administrative costs. The findings provide evidence to support the 
strategic amalgamation of small schemes into larger, diversified risk pools. The purpose will be to improve financial 
resilience, reduce administrative costs, strengthen risk pooling, and support the long-term sustainability of the 
private healthcare financing sector.
Outcome 4 Stakeholder engagement at local, regional, and international level
Progress Review 
Statement
During the reporting period, the CMS convened 33 direct stakeholder engagements focused on regulatory 
matters, strategic collaboration opportunities, and key organisational initiatives. These engagements strengthened 
relationships with stakeholders and promoted ongoing dialogue on issues affecting the medical schemes industry.
At the regional level, the CMS convened an engagement with the Namibia Financial Institutions Supervisory 
Authority (NAMFISA) to strengthen regulatory cooperation and facilitate the exchange of best practice.
The CMS further strengthened collaboration with local stakeholders through the signing of Memoranda of 
Understanding (MOUs). This provides a framework for cooperation in areas such as research, policy development, 
regulatory oversight, compliance, consumer protection, and institutional capacity building. These partnerships 
support knowledge sharing, coordinated initiatives, and the achievement of the organisation’s strategic objectives.
Outcome 5 Good governance and ethical leadership
Progress Review 
Statement
During the 2025/26 financial year, the CMS continued to strengthen good governance and ethical leadership. 
This was achieved through effective oversight, sound organisational management, and compliance with legislative 
requirements. The Council and its committees met regularly to provide strategic direction and oversight on key 
organisational priorities, ensuring that the organisation continued to fulfil its mandate effectively.
In line with the PFMA, the CMS submitted all required quarterly financial and non-financial performance reports 
to the Executive Authority and the Department of Planning, Monitoring and Evaluation (DPME).  By doing so, it 
demonstrated strong organisational performance against its strategic objectives.   A small number of performance 
targets were not achieved due to technological challenges and the loss of institutional knowledge following 
critical staff resignations and retirements. However, corrective measures have been implemented to address these 
challenges.
The CMS further promoted an ethical organisational culture through the publication of Ethics Bulletins, the 
implementation of an Ethics Awareness and Assessment Survey, and the review of the Fraud and Corruption 
Prevention Policy. The purpose was to strengthen integrity, accountability, and internal controls. Organisational 
capacity was further enhanced through strategic leadership appointments, including the Chief Financial Officer 
(CFO), the Executive Manager: Office of the Chief Executive and Registrar, and the Senior Manager: Audit and Risk. 
These appointments strengthened governance, financial management, and assurance functions.
In addition, the CMS invested in employee development through the submission and implementation of the 
Workplace Skills Plan and Annual Training Report. At the same time, it supported employee well-being through 
a comprehensive wellness programme that provides ongoing psychological support, counselling services, and 
wellness awareness initiatives.
52
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Outcome 6 Conducting policy-driven research, and the monitoring and evaluation of the medical schemes industry to 
facilitate decision-making and policy recommendations to the Health Ministry.
Progress Review 
Statement
The CMS has published research in peer-reviewed journals and participated in industry events and conferences, 
including the Value-Based Care Annual Conference. It also  contributed a book chapter on primary healthcare 
focusing on public and private sector partnerships. The CMS has also partnered with the South African Medical 
Research Council to strengthen the evidence-based development of benefit definition guidelines using the GRADE-
Adolopment framework.  The latter integrates adoption, adaptation, and de novo development of clinical guidelines.
The CMS is in the process of revising the PMB package with an emphasis on including more primary health care 
benefits. The inclusion of primary health care in the PMBs as part of the review process will lead to the overall 
improvement of the National Health Outcomes. Current developments in health technology assessments are being 
addressed through the development and publication of PMB Definition Guidelines. It utilises evidence-informed 
decision-making processes and methodologies to define PMB entitlements for beneficiaries through a stakeholder 
consultative process.
The role that the CMS will play towards the achievement of this NDP mandate is the collection, analysis, and 
provision of private health quality data. In addition, the PMB definition, which is a key part of the PMB review, 
will ensure that there is an improved understanding by scheme members of their benefits and entitlements. The 
inclusion of the Primary Healthcare (including TB) in the PMBs as part of the review process will lead to the overall 
improvement of the National Health Outcomes. 
Refocusing research efforts and aligning them with policy developments across the public and private health sectors 
will be essential to achieving this outcome. This will require reprioritising research and analytical outputs across 
relevant programmes to strengthen collaboration, maximise synergies, and eliminate duplication of effort. Emphasis 
should be placed on producing evidence-based, policy-relevant, and high-impact research that addresses emerging 
health system priorities and informs regulatory and policy decision-making. Through partnerships with academic 
institutions, research organisations, and other key stakeholders, these research initiatives should generate credible 
evidence. The evidence should support effective regulation, contribute to improvements in the health system, and 
enhance stakeholders’ understanding of the evolving healthcare environment.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
The information related to institutional programme performance spans four programmes: Programme 1: Administration; 
Programme 2: Regulation; Programme 3: Member Protection; and Programme 4: Policy, Research, and Monitoring. 
Each programme will be discussed in detail below.
Programme 1: Administration
The administrative programmes of the Council for Medical Schemes focus on the efficient functioning of the office and 
provide support to the core programmes to effectively carry out their mandates. The administration programme entails 
five sub-programmes, namely:
Sub-programme 1.1: Office of the Chief Executive and Registrar
Sub-programme 1.2: Office of the Chief Financial Officer
Sub-programme 1.3: Information Communication Technology and Information Management
Sub-programme 1.4: Corporate Services
Sub-programme 1.5: Council Secretariat
Sub-programme 1.1: Office of the Chief Executive and Registrar
The CEO is the accounting officer exercising overall control over the office of the CMS, and as Registrar, has legislated 
powers to regulate medical schemes, administrators, brokers, and managed care organisations. The CEO and Registrar 
is responsible for leading the development and execution of the CMS’ strategy. The CEO and Registrar is ultimately 
responsible for all day-to-day management decisions and for implementing the CMS’ strategic and annual plans.

Sub-programme 1.2: Office of the Chief Financial Officer
The purpose of this sub-programme is to serve all business units in the CMS, the executive management team, and the 
Council. It will do so by maintaining an efficient, effective, and transparent system of financial performance and supply 
chain management that complies with the applicable legislation. The Office of the CFO, in support of the Registrar, also

South Africa. It makes available to them information and reports that allow them to carry out statutory responsibilities.

Strategy to overcome areas of under-performance
In order to overcome areas of under-performance, the CMS will proactively engage the NDoH and National Treasury 
to expedite the finalisation and approval of the Terms of Reference for the funding model review. Following the 
appointment of a service provider, the CMS will provide technical expertise, financial information, and strategic input. 
The purpose will be to support the development of a sustainable funding model aligned to the organisation’s long-
term objectives. Implementation progress will be closely monitored through structured engagements with the NDoH to 
facilitate timely decision-making, address implementation risks, and ensure delivery within the revised project timelines.

Sub-programme 1.5: Council Secretariat
The purpose of this sub-programme is to provide corporate governance services to the Council as Accounting Author-
ity and its committees. The Council Secretariat also provides support to the independent Appeals Board and ensures 
that all rulings are communicated to key stakeholders. The programme seeks to achieve the above objective through 
seamless board administration, secretariat service, and support.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Programme 2: Regulation
The purpose of the Programme is to: 
• Ensure that brokers and broker organisations, administrators, and managed care organisations are accredited in 
line with the accreditation requirements as set out in the Medical Schemes Act (131  of 1998). This includes whether 
applicants are fit and proper, have the necessary resources, skills, capacity and infrastructure, and are financially 
sound. 
• To serve beneficiaries of medical schemes and the public in general by reviewing and approving changes to   
contributions paid by members and benefits offered by schemes. The Programme analyses and approves all scheme 
rules to ensure consistency with the MSA. This ensures that the beneficiaries have access to affordable and appropriate 
quality health care. By doing this, we help the CMS ensure that the rules of medical schemes are fair to beneficiaries 
and are consistent with the MSA. 
• Serve members of medical schemes and the public in general by taking appropriate action to enforce compliance  
with the Medical Schemes Act. 
• To monitor and regulate the overall financial performance of medical schemes in line with the financial requirements 
of the MSA, through the analysis of financial information.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Strategy to overcome areas of under-performance
Benefits Management
As far as benefits management is concerned, the sub-
programme is responsible for the processing of scheme 
rules, the monitoring of marketing material, and the 
provision of guidance on contribution increases and 
benefit changes. These functions contribute to the 
achievement of the CMS’ mandate of regulating medical 
schemes in accordance with the MSA and protecting the 
interests of beneficiaries.
Medical scheme operations, including contributions, 
benefits, and governance arrangements, are governed 
by registered rules. The sub-programme therefore plays 
a critical role in ensuring that rule amendments are 
consistent with the MSA and support fair and equitable 
access to benefits.
During the 2025/26 financial year, the sub-programme 
achieved and exceeded most of its planned targets.
The target relating to the review and publication of Model 
Rules was not achieved due to extended legal and quality 
assurance processes required to ensure alignment with 
the Medical Schemes Act and regulatory requirements.
To address the under-performance, the CMS will allocate 
dedicated legal and technical resources, introduce a 
milestone-driven review process and initiate stakeholder 
engagement earlier. Quality assurance will also be 
integrated earlier in the drafting process, supported by 
enhanced monitoring against clearly defined timelines, to 
minimise delays and reduce rework.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Programme 3: Member Protection
The purpose of this Programme is to:
 
• Provide customer service and training in support of the CMS’ stakeholder engagement initiatives. 
• Serve the beneficiaries of medical schemes and the public by investigating and resolving complaints in an efficient  
and effective manner which is clinically appropriate and based on evidence. By doing this, we ensure that beneficiaries 
are treated fairly, and that medical schemes and other regulated entities are compliant with the Act,  Regulations, 
and registered scheme rules. 
• Provide support to the office on clinical matters so that good quality medical scheme cover is maximised and that  
regulated entities are properly governed through prospective and retrospective regulation, with inputs to benefits 
design. This will ensure that the policies and protocols applied are aligned with best practice and the local South 
African standards, taking into consideration the affordability and sustainability of schemes.

quality.

Complaints

the development of a new complaints system was started 
and it is envisaged that the new system, once procured 
would alleviate the ongoing system downtimes. In the 
interim, the sub-programme relies on ICT support to 
troubleshoot and resolve existing and emerging system 
errors as they arise.
 
The Unit has advertised the position of Legal Adjudication 
Officer, to fill the vacancy which opened after the internal 
appointment of the new Senior Legal Adjudication 
Officer. Once this position has been filled, the Unit will 
have addressed the under-performance which was caused 
by the staff shortage.

Programme 4: Policy, Research, and Monitoring
The purpose of the Programme is to:
• The purpose of the Programme is to serve beneficiaries of medical schemes and members of the public. This will 
be achieved by collecting and analysing data to monitor, evaluate, and report on trends in medical schemes. It will 
also conduct research, measure risk in medical schemes, and develop recommendations to improve regulatory 
policy and practice. By doing this, the programme helps the CMS to contribute to the development of policy that 
enhances the protection of the interest of beneficiaries and members of the public. 
• The Programme also undertakes strategic research that enables the CMS to advise the NDoH on policy initiatives 
such as the NHI.

The allocated budgets have enabled the Unit to advance its policy research agenda and meet its strategic targets 
in line with Section 7 of the Medical Schemes Act. This particularly applies to protecting beneficiaries’ interests (7a), 
coordinating scheme functions with the national health policy (7b), and recommending the quality and outcomes 
measures (7c). It also applies to collecting and sharing private healthcare data (7e), advising the Minister on medical 
scheme matters (7g), and performing other mandated functions (7h). 
Specifically, the budget has supported continued progress on the review of PMBs, the development and costing of the 
Primary Healthcare (PHC) package, and the formulation of a base benefit package. It has also supported continued 
progress on the standardisation of benefit options. All of these are critical to promoting the affordability, comparability, 
and equity within the medical scheme’s environment. Continued financial support will maintain momentum toward 
evidence-based reforms and regulatory innovations. Further support has also been provided to the NDoH for the 
implementation of other HMI Recommendations. This includes the development of the draft Regulations for Pricing 
and providing support to the Fraud Waste, Abuse, and Error (FWAE) activities with participation in the FWAE Advisory 
committee and technical workstreams.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Introduction
The Council is appointed by the Minister of Health and is 
responsible for the strategic oversight of the entity. It 
approves the 5-Year Strategic Plan, the Annual 
Performance Plans, the Internal Control Framework as 
well as the policies of the organisation. The Council 
operates on the basis of an approved Charter. All six 
committees have approved Charters. 
The Charters complement the existing legislative 
instruments such as the: 
• Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998) (MSA)
• Public Finance Management Act (1 of 1991) (PFMA) 
• National Treasury Regulations
• Inspections of Financial Institutions Act (80 of 1998)
Further, the Council draws its governance philosophy 
from the King IV Code of Corporate Governance in South 
Africa. The Council will, during the course of 2026 be 
trained in the new code, King V.  It views governance to 
embody such principles and practices as ethical 
leadership, effective control, monitoring and evaluation, 
and ethical culture.
Portfolio Committees 
During the reporting period, the CMS engaged with the 
Portfolio Committee on Health on key governance 
matters. The Annual Report for 2024/25 was presented 
on 9 October 2025, and the Annual Performance Plan 
(APP) for 2025/26 was presented in January 2026 and was 
approved. The Council met with the Portfolio Committee 
on the following dates:
• 23 April 2025 
• 9 October 2025
• 25 March 2026
• 30 April 2026
Executive Authority 
In terms of the PFMA, the Council is required to submit 
quarterly reports to the Executive Authority. These reports 
range from financial management, performance 
information, and strategic risk management, to 
compliance. All reports were submitted timeously during 
the reporting period as follows as per the requirements of 
the PFMA:
• Quarter 1 – 31 July 2025
• Quarter 2 – 31 October 2025
• Quarter 3 – 31 January 2026
• Quarter 4 – 30 April 2026
There were no issues of concern raised by the Executive 
Authority regarding the reports submitted by the Council. 
The Accounting Authority
The accounting authority of the CMS is the Council. The 
Minister of Health appoints up to 15 members to the 
Council, selected from diverse sectors of society and 
bringing expertise in areas such as accounting, economics, 
medicine, law, and civil society. In terms of the MSA, the 
Council is responsible for:
• Protecting the beneficiaries of medical schemes.
• Controlling and coordinating the functioning of   
 medical schemes.
• Advising the Minister of Health on the quality and   
 outcomes of relevant health services provided by   
 medical schemes.
• Investigating complaints and resolving disputes, and
• Collecting and disseminating information about the   
 private healthcare industry.
Beyond its statutory responsibilities, the Council also 
carries out its broader governance oversight function by:
• Evaluating and approving the five-year Strategic Plan.
• Evaluating and approving the Annual Performance   
 Plan. 
• Evaluating and approving the annual financial   
 statements and annual performance information   
 report.
• Exercising oversight of the executive management’s   
 performance.
The Council exercises its functions in terms of the Medical 
Schemes Act, the Public Finance Management Act, 
Treasury regulations, other applicable laws, and its charter 
and code of conduct. Further, its work is carried out by 
various committees that report directly to it. The Chief 
Executive Officer (CEO) and Registrar is accountable to 
the Council for his duties.
Council Charter
The Council has adopted a formal Charter to regulate its 
conduct. The Charter outlines its responsibilities for 
approving strategy, monitoring executive management 
performance, ensuring effective risk management and 
internal controls, and overseeing the implementation of 
approved policies. 
The Council’s Charter is reviewed on an annual basis 
together with the Committee’s Charters. The Council is 
satisfied that it has complied with its Charter. The recent 
Council evaluation conducted by an external independent 
service provider gave the Council a score of more than 
80% in terms of its performance as a Council. This is 
testimony to its comprehensive Charter that is strictly 
complied with at all times.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Risk Management 
The CMS is mandated to regulate the medical schemes industry and protect the interests of beneficiaries. To effectively 
discharge this mandate, CMS continuously monitors and manages strategic risks that may affect the achievement of its 
objectives. During the 2025/26 financial year, focus was placed on managing the implications of National Health 
Insurance (NHI) developments on the medical scheme’s environment, as well as advancing technology and digital 
transformation initiatives to strengthen institutional resilience, improve decision-making, and enhance service delivery. 
 
During the reporting period, the Council conducted a strategic risk assessment to identify potential risks and 
opportunities related to the strategic outcomes articulated in the Strategic Plan (2025–2030). The identified strategic 
risks were systematically mapped to the CMS’ strategic outcomes within the strategic risk register. This alignment 
ensures clear line-of-sight between strategy, risk exposure and management action, thereby strengthening accountability 
and enabling more effective monitoring and reporting at both Council and Management levels. The assessment 
identified the following 10 strategic risk exposures, listed in order of significance in the table below:
Risk

83
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
The overall strategic risk profile of the CMS reflects an 
increase in risk exposure, positioning the institution within 
the medium-risk category. This movement indicates 
increased exposure to strategic risks and highlights the 
need for continued focus on control effectiveness, risk 
management practices, and institutional capacity to 
identify, assess, and respond to emerging risks.
The stability of certain high-impact risks, combined with 
reductions in others, demonstrates that the CMS is 
increasingly proactive in mitigating key exposures while 
maintaining focus on its strategic priorities. Importantly, 
this improvement suggests that risk management is 
becoming more embedded as a strategic enabler rather 
than a compliance exercise, supporting better decision-
making and execution.
The CMS strives to create a risk-aware culture that filters 
throughout the institution. This is done by introducing a 
continuous risk management process. The latter includes 
managing risk incidents, implementing action plans, 
conducting daily risk management activities and 
monitoring and assessing external risks, including those 
arising due to climate change. The Council and ARC are 
continuously advised on the risk management processes 
within the CMS. The CMS has an approved Enterprise 
Risk Management policy and framework which includes 
the organisation’s risk appetite and risk tolerance. In the 
future the CMS will define its risk tolerance levels, and 
they will be monitored and reported to the relevant 
governance processes. 
Risk Champions are appointed in each unit to raise 
awareness and support the Executive Management 
Committee and the Chief Executive and Registrar in 
implementing and reporting on risk management 
activities. This is achieved by aligning efforts and 
coordinating the risk management activities across the 
organisation. Such coordination will ensure a standard 
risk management practice in line with the Enterprise Risk 
Management Framework.
Business Continuity Management 
During the period, the CMS established a consistent  
Business Continuity Management (BCM) process, 
covering both the development and execution of plans. 
The aim of BCM is to strengthen resilience in the day-to-
day business operations, but also to ensure the effective 
management of unexpected disruptive events. Business 
disruptions can affect any organisation and arises from 
factors such as adverse weather, political unrest, hazardous 
spillages, power outages, or pandemics.
To mitigate these risks the CMS has developed Business 
Continuity Plans (BCPs) and Business Impact Analysis 
(BIAs). The BIAs and BCPs have been reviewed annually, 
or as circumstances require. In addition, the CMS has 
conducted the penetration testing and vulnerability 
assessment during the prior year and is busy preparing 
for the cyber risk management maturity assessment for 
this financial year. Continuous disaster recovery testing 
has been conducted to ensure business continuity, 
prevent devastating financial losses, and minimise 
prolonged downtime. 
The CMS has integrated its BCM policy, ICT backups and 
disaster recovery policy.  There is a BCM committee in 
place to ensure that:
• There is a developed, reviewed and implemented BCM 
framework in place.
• Critical business functions and associated risks are 
identified.
• There will be a contribution to the development and  
testing of BCPs.
• Business impact analyses and risk assessments will be 
supported.
• There will be reporting on continuity readiness within 
their respective divisions or functions.  
Internal Control Unit
Combined Assurance
In accordance with corporate governance best practices, 
combined assurance is defined as that which  ‘Incorporates 
and optimises all assurance services and functions so 
that, taken as a whole, these enable an effective control 
environment; support the integrity of information used 
for internal decision-making by management, the 
governing body and its committees; and support the 
integrity of the organisation’s external reports.
The CMS is in the process of implementing a combined 
assurance process, which affirms the organisation’s 
commitment to good governance practices. The 
combined assurance model integrates the efforts of 
internal assurance providers with those of independent 
and external assurance providers in addressing the 
significant risk areas affecting the organisation. There is a 
combined assurance framework and universe in place 
that will be monitored by the ARC to ensure the 
effectiveness of the organisation’s combined assurance 
process. The combined assurance plan will focus on 
business processes, systems, and controls aligned with 
the CMS’ strategic objectives. The plan will demonstrate 
commitment to enhancing risk management and ensuring 
the effectiveness of operational controls. 
Internal Audit
 
An internal audit is an independent, objective assurance 
and consulting activity designed to add value and 
improve the CMS’ operations. It helps the CMS to achieve 
its objectives by evaluating the effectiveness of internal 
controls, risk management, and governance processes.
In line with the CMS’ service delivery model, the internal 
audit function, which encompasses the evaluation and 
review of internal controls, is outsourced to an external 
service provider. This approach ensures access to 
independent expertise, enhanced objectivity, and a 
scalability of resources to meet evolving organisational 
needs.
84
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Lunika Inc. was initially appointed as the outsourced 
internal audit service provider for a three-year term, 
commencing on 1 April 2021 and concluding on 31 March 
2024. Following a competitive service provider selection 
process, conducted in accordance with the CMS’ 
procurement policies and governance requirements, 
Lunika Inc. was reappointed for a further three-year term. 
This was effective from 1 April 2024 and will be concluding 
on 31 March 2027.
Based on this strategic view, the internal audit service 
provider also developed an Annual Internal Audit Plan. 
This annual plan outlines the detailed scope, estimated 
cost, and projected timelines for each audit engagement 
scheduled for the year. The plan is subject to review and 
approval by the ARC.
As part of its mandate, the service provider delivers audit 
reports to the ARC after each engagement. These reports 
detail the outcomes of the audits, key findings, 
recommendations, and management responses. The 
reports also provide an update on the provider’s 
performance against the approved Annual Audit Plan, 
ensuring transparency and enabling the ARC to exercise 
its oversight responsibilities effectively.
In carrying out audits, the scope of work of an internal 
audit is to determine whether the CMS’ network of risk 
management, control systems, and governance processes 
is adequate. It also assesses whether these processes, as 
designed and represented by management, are 
functioning in an effective manner to provide reasonable 
assurance that:
• Significant risks relating to the achievement of the CMS’ 
strategic objectives are appropriately identified and 
managed. Interaction with the various governance 
groups within the organisation occurs as needed.
• Significant financial, operational, managerial, 
performance, and information technology information 
is accurate, reliable, and timely.
• The actions of the CMS’ employees follow the CMS’ 
policies, procedures, and applicable laws, regulations, 
and governance standards.
• Resources and assets are acquired and disposed of 
economically, used efficiently, and protected adequately.
• The results of operations or programmes are consistent 
with the established goals and objectives  of the 
CMS and are being carried out effectively and efficiently. 
• Established processes and systems enable compliance 
with the policies, procedures, laws, and regulations 
that could significantly impact the CMS.
• Information and the means used to identify, measure, 
analyse, classify, and report such information are reliable 
and have integrity.
• The CMS’ fraud prevention plan is implemented and 
operates effectively and efficiently; and
• The assets, revenue, income, and interests of the CMS 
are accounted for and are safeguarded against fraud, 
corruption,losses of all kinds, wastage, inefficient 
administration, and any other problems  that may 
occur.
To achieve full effectiveness, the scope of the work that 
was performed by the internal audit, was based on its 
assessment of risk (with management input) as approved 
by the ARC. Audit coverage was also based on a risk 
basis and any other areas as directed and approved by 
the ARC. In 2022, an assessment regarding the internal 
audit service provider’s conformance to the International 
Standards for the Professional Practice of Internal Auditors 
was conducted.  As per the standards the external 
assessments are conducted at least once every five years 
by a qualified, independent assessor or assessment team 
from outside the organisation. 
There were 11 audits planned for the 2025/26 financial 
year, three assurance services, one advisory service and 
seven reviews were planned by the internal audit. As part 
of the reviews, the performance information review 
consisted of four quarterly reviews and the probity reviews 
consisted of six probity reviews.
