Audit and Risk Committee 
The Committee reports that it has discharged its fiduciary 
duties arising from sections 50(1) and 51(1) of the PFMA 
and Treasury Regulations based on the Committee’s 
critical oversight role. The ARC has adopted appropriate 
formal terms of reference in its ARC Charter, regulated its 
affairs in compliance with this Charter and discharged all 
its responsibilities as contained therein. The Committee 
oversees all internal audit, finance, risk management, 
regulatory compliance, performance information, and 
whistleblowing matters. The Council remains ultimately 
accountable for financial information, performance 
information, regulatory compliance, and risk management.
The Committee was chaired by Dr Masibulele Phesa from 
16 February 2023. It is important to note that the 
committee member’s term was due to end on 28 February 
2026, but was subsequently extended by one month to 
the end of March 2026. Mr Itani Phaduli then took over 
the Chairperson role. The Chief Executive and Registrar, 
the Executive in the Chief Executive’s Office, the Chief 
Financial Officer and the Senior Manager: Audit and Risk, 
and the Auditor-General of South Africa have standing 
invitations to the ARC meetings. 
The ARC is responsible to ensure that the Internal Audit 
Function performs their responsibilities effectively and 
efficiently by: 
• Reviewing and approving the Internal Audit Charter.
• Reviewing audit results and proposed corrective action 
plans of management.
• Reviewing the plans and budgets of the Internal Audit 
Function. Ensure that the plan addresses the high-risk 
areas and that adequate resources are available to 
allow for independence and an effective internal audit 
effort.
• Requesting ad hoc audit projects when and if deemed 
necessary.
• Reviewing the effectiveness of the internal audit 
function annually.
• Providing support for communication with management, 
the Auditor-General and other assurance providers.
• Ensuring direct access of the internal audit function  
either to the ARC or its Chairperson in closed meeting 
as is necessary.
• Ensuring that internal audit work is co-ordinated with 
the Auditor-General to ensure little or no duplication  
of work and coverage, including other assurance   
providers.
• Receiving and reviewing quarterly progress reports 
submitted by the Internal Auditor.
In addition, the Audit and Risk Committee has the 
responsibility to:
• Oversee that the CMS carries out its responsibilities.
• Oversee that management implements  the necessary 
controls to enable compliance with pertinent laws and 
regulations. 
• Oversee that management is conducting its affairs 
ethically and is maintaining effective controls against 
conflicts of interest and fraud.
• Support the functions of the Auditor-General in relation 
to the CMS.
• Ensure effective Enterprise Risk Management, 
Performance Information Management and reporting 
systems, values and ethics practices, organisational 
governance, Fraud and Protection of Information, a 
Fraud and Corruption Prevention Strategy, and 
Combined Assurance. 
To fulfil its responsibilities and duties in respect of 
Financial Statements the ARC is responsible for:
• Reviewing the CMS’ annual financial statements and  
any reports or other financial information submitted 
to  any governmental body, stakeholders or the public. 
This includes any certification, report, opinion, or  
review rendered by the independent accountants or  
other consultants.
• Reviewing the integrity of the organisation’s financial 
reporting processes in consultation with the external  
auditors and the internal auditors. Considering the 
independent auditors’ judgements about the quality  
and appropriateness of the CMS’ accounting policies, 
and principles as applied in its financial reporting.
• Considering and recommending, if appropriate, major 
changes to the CMS’ auditing and accounting  
principles and practices. This should be in line with 
the  suggestions by the management of the internal 
audit function, the Auditor General or audit-related  
consultants e.g. IT.
• Be satisfied that the annual financial statements fairly 
present the state of affairs of the CMS, its business, its 
financial results, and its position as at the end of the  
financial year concerned. Also be satisfied that they 
fairly represent the CMS’ performance against pre-
determined objectives.
• Reporting to the Accounting Authority any matter it   
considers significant.
Audit and Risk Committee Reporting Responsibilities 
include: 
• Regularly reporting to the Accounting Authority about 
the Committee’s activities, issues, and related  
recommendations. This includes reporting about the 
quarterly financial and performance reports from 
management which are for issue to the Executive 
Authority and National Treasury after being reviewed 
and authorised by the Accounting Authority.
• Reporting annually to the Executive Authority, 
describing the committee’s composition, responsibilities 
and how they were discharged, and any other 
information required, including approval of non-audit 
services.
• Reviewing any other reports regarding the CMS’ issues 
that relate to the Committee’s responsibilities.
• Providing an annual ARC report for inclusion in the 
Annual Report related to the achievement of this 
Charter’s responsibilities and other matters required 
by law or because of their nature or significance.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Compliance with Laws and 
Regulations  
The PFMA section 51(1)(h) states that an Accounting 
Authority for a public entity must comply with the 
provisions of this Act and any other legislation applicable 
to the public entity. It must also ensure compliance by the 
public entity with the aforementioned legislation. The 
CMS has established and implemented an approved 
comprehensive Regulatory Compliance Policy and 
Framework; and Regulatory Compliance Universe to 
guide its adherence to applicable legislative, regulatory, 
and best practice requirements. Quarterly compliance 
reports were presented to the ARC and the Council on 
the level of compliance with applicable legislation 
particularly the PFMA. 
There are 24 pieces of legislation that have been identified 
as relevant to the CMS and these legislations were 
categorised in terms of core, secondary, and topical. Four 
core legislations that directly affect the CMS were 
identified as follows:
• Public Finance Management Act (1 of 1999)  (National 
Treasury Regulations)
• Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998) Section 13 (2)
• CMS Levies Act (58 of 2000)
• Protection of Personal Information Act (4 of 2013) 
In strengthening regulatory compliance, detailed 
Compliance Risk Management Plans (CRMPs) were 
developed for core legislations, delivering a structured 
view of obligations, inherent risks, control activities, and 
accountability matrices.
In the forthcoming year the CMS will advance its 
regulatory compliance framework by conducting 
compliance monitoring and increasing awareness on 
legal compliance. This will demonstrate the CMS’ 
commitment to legal compliance which demonstrates 
ethical leadership, operational resilience, and sustained 
public confidence. 
Fraud and Corruption 
Whistleblowing
In compliance with the Protected Disclosures Act (26 of 
2000) the CMS has established and maintains an 
independent fraud hotline service. The hotline service is 
managed by an independent service provider. The service 
provider was appointed on 2 June 2025. Having an 
independent service provider ensures that there is 
guaranteed anonymity and it safeguards the CMS against 
legal liability. It also encourages higher reporting rates. It 
removes internal bias, detects fraud early, and 
demonstrates ethical accountability to stakeholders. 
During the year under review, all concerns or complaints 
received, whether from within or outside the CMS, were 
received, recorded, analysed, and allocated to relevant 
units for investigation. The Social and Ethics, and Audit 
and Risk Committees received and discussed reports 
from the Whistleblowing Hotline that were presented by 
the Audit and Risk unit. 
Minimising Conflict of Interest 
The CMS has implemented a structured and proactive 
system to manage conflicts of interest among both staff 
and Council members. As part of this system, all 
employees and Council members are required to submit 
annual declarations of interest. These declarations are 
reviewed and assessed to identify any potential or actual 
conflicts of interest that may compromise the integrity of 
the CMS’ operations.
To reinforce this process, the Supply Chain Management 
(SCM) unit conducts an independent verification of 
entities and individuals conducting business with the 
CMS through the National Treasury’s Central Supplier 
Database (CSD). This ensures that suppliers and service 
providers do not have undisclosed relationships with the 
CMS personnel, thereby reducing the risk of corruption 
or improper influence in procurement decisions.
In instances where a conflict of interest is identified, 
members of the relevant bid evaluation or adjudication 
committees are required to recuse themselves from the 
procurement process. This measure upholds the principles 
of transparency, fairness, and accountability, and ensures 
the integrity of the CMS procurement and decision-
making processes.
Code of Conduct 
The CMS has formally adopted a comprehensive Code of 
Ethics and Conduct. It remains in full effect as the 
foundational framework guiding the ethical behaviour of 
all the CMS employees, suppliers, and members of the 
Accounting Authority. This Code establishes the uniform 
ethical principles and standards that govern professional 
conduct, ensuring that all actions and decisions reflect 
CMS’ core values of integrity, accountability, transparency, 
and professionalism.
The Code of Ethics and Conduct is periodically reviewed 
to maintain relevance and effectiveness. The most recent 
review was conducted in consultation with a broad range 
of internal stakeholders, including management, staff 
representatives, and governance bodies. This review 
process aligns with the Council’s annual Ethics Awareness 
Outlook, which seeks to embed a strong ethical culture 
throughout the organisation. Concurrently, the CMS 
continues to implement its Ethics Strategy and Awareness 
Plan, aimed at enhancing ethical understanding and 
behaviour at all organisational levels. To facilitate this, all 
staff members receive a detailed CMS Ethics Booklet, 
which serves as a practical guide to expected conduct 
and decision-making in various workplace scenarios.
88
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Framework for Addressing Breaches of the 
Code of Conduct
The CMS has established a clear and structured 
disciplinary framework to address breaches of the Code 
of Ethics and Conduct. The framework distinguishes 
between less serious and serious offences, ensuring 
proportionality, fairness, and due process in managing 
misconduct.
Less Serious Offences
When a breach is deemed to be of a less serious nature, 
the CMS management initiates an informal disciplinary 
hearing. The following key features apply:
• The process typically involves only the employee 
concerned and their immediate supervisor or manager.
• The objective is to address the issue swiftly, promote 
corrective behaviour, and prevent recurrence without 
the need for formal sanctions.
• Possible outcomes include a verbal or a written 
warning, depending on the severity and circumstances 
of the breach.
• Documentation of the warning is maintained for 
reference but is generally less formal than a full 
disciplinary record.
This informal approach encourages open communication, 
remediation, and an early resolution of minor breaches.
Serious Offences
For breaches classified as serious, the CMS applies a 
more formal disciplinary process to uphold organisational 
standards and accountability. This process includes:
• Convening a formal disciplinary hearing, convened 
by a chairperson appointed by the Registrar.
• Appointment of an initiator who presents the case 
on  behalf of the CMS, ensuring that the facts and  
evidence are clearly articulated.
• The accused employee’s right to representation by a 
fellow CMS employee or a recognised shop steward.
The employee is entitled to:
• Present their case and provide evidence in their 
defence. Cross-examine witnesses who testify against 
them.
• Make submissions in mitigation before any sanction is 
imposed.
• Potential sanctions for a guilty verdict include a final  
written warning, suspension without pay, or 
dismissal,  depending on the gravity of the offence 
and any mitigating or aggravating factors.
This formal disciplinary procedure is conducted in strict 
adherence to principles of natural justice, ensuring that 
the employee receives a fair hearing and that decisions 
are based on an objective assessment of facts.
Governance and Oversight
The disciplinary framework forms a critical component of 
the CMS’ broader governance and ethics programme. 
The Registrar, supported by the HR and legal units, 
oversees the implementation of disciplinary processes to 
ensure consistency and compliance with labour 
legislation and the CMS’ policy. Outcomes of disciplinary 
hearings, particularly those involving serious breaches, 
are reported to the ARC and the Accounting Authority as 
part of the organisation’s commitment to transparency 
and accountability.
Continuous Ethics Development
To strengthen and sustain a culture of ethical conduct, 
the CMS complements its disciplinary framework with a 
range of preventative and developmental ethics 
initiatives. These initiatives are designed not only to 
promote awareness but also to equip employees with 
the practical tools necessary to recognise, address, and 
appropriately respond to ethical challenges in the 
workplace.
As part of these efforts, the CMS provides staff with the 
CMS Ethics Booklet, which outlines expected standards 
of conduct, practical guidance on ethical decision-
making, and procedures for reporting misconduct. In 
addition, the CMS regularly facilitates ethics workshops 
and training sessions, tailored to promote an 
understanding of the Code of Ethics and Conduct and its 
application to real-world scenarios. These sessions 
encourage open dialogue, clarify ethical expectations, 
and support employees in aligning their behaviour with 
the CMS’ values.
During the period under review, the CMS also undertook 
a formal Ethics Assessment Exercise to evaluate the 
current ethical climate within the organisation. The 
assessment aimed to identify strengths, areas for 
improvement, and potential ethical risks. The results of 
this assessment are currently pending and will inform 
future ethics strategies, interventions, and policy 
enhancements once finalised.
These initiatives reflect the CMS’ ongoing commitment 
to ethical governance, the promotion of integrity, 
accountability, and professionalism across all levels.
Health, Safety & Environmental Issues
In accordance with the Occupational Health and Safety 
Act (85 of 1993), the CMS established an Occupational 
Health and Safety Committee. Its role is to promote and 
improve occupational health and safety in the workplace. 
Their primary function is to advise and support the 
employer in implementing and improving health and 
safety, leading to a safer and healthier work environment. 
The committee participates in identifying and controlling 
workplace hazards through assessments and the 
development of control programmes to ensure employee 
safety.
89
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Council Secretariat
The Council Secretariat continued to provide governance 
support and advisory services to the Council and its 
committees during the year under review, ensuring 
compliance with applicable legislation, governance 
frameworks, and Council-approved policies. The Council 
Secretariat facilitated the effective functioning of the 
Council and committee meetings, provided guidance on 
governance matters, and supported the implementation 
of resolutions arising from the Council’s deliberations.
In line with the Council’s commitment to continuous 
governance improvement, the Council Secretariat 
coordinated induction and development initiatives for 
members. The Council Secretariat also  provided ongoing 
updates on legislative, regulatory, and governance 
developments affecting the medical schemes 
environment. The Council remains satisfied that the 
Council Secretariat maintains an appropriate level of 
independence and performs the role effectively. The 
Council has faith that the Council Secretariat possesses 
the requisite qualifications, experience, and competence 
to support the Council in the execution of its oversight 
responsibilities.
Social Responsibility 
During the reporting period, the CMS’ employees 
supported a social responsibility initiative aimed at 
providing school shoes to learners at Olivenhoutbosch 
Primary School in Centurion. Feedback from educators 
indicated that  several learners lacked proper footwear, 
which contributed to instances of absenteeism. In 
response, the staff donated more than 50 pairs of shoes 
during the 2025/2026 school year, and these were 
distributed to beneficiaries during the winter period.

and professional standards.

The Audit and Risk Committee comprised five members,

Committee was appropriately constituted, with a diverse 
and relevant mix of qualifications, professional expertise 
and experience in accounting, auditing, finance, 
governance, risk management, procurement and business 
administration. This composition supported an appropriate 
balance of independence, institutional knowledge, and 
oversight in the execution of the Committee’s 
responsibilities.
The Committee was chaired by Dr Masibulele Phesa 
during the year under review. Dr Phesa served as 
Chairperson from 16 February 2023. His term, which was 
due to end on 28 February 2026, was subsequently

Audit and Risk Committee Focus Areas
Effectiveness of Internal Control
The ARC considered the adequacy and effectiveness of 
the CMS’ internal control environment based on reports 
received from management and Internal Audit, the 
organisation’s risk management processes and the results 
of the external audit.
The Internal Audit Plan was risk-based and provided 
assurance across key areas of governance, risk 
management, internal control, financial and performance 
information, ICT, regulatory compliance, fraud prevention 
and the safeguarding of organisational resources and 
assets.
During the year under review, the CMS continued to 
review and strengthen its policies and control environment 
to support effective governance and mitigate the risk of 
mismanagement, fraud and corruption.
The overall assessment of the CMS’ internal control, 
governance, and risk management environment was 
satisfactory. Assurance providers provided assurance on 
the adequacy and effectiveness of key controls, 
governance processes, and risk management practices. 
Where areas of weakness or opportunities for improvement 
were identified, appropriate recommendations were 
made to address the identified deficiencies, strengthen 
the control environment, enhance governance and risk 
management practices, and support the CMS’ commitment 
to continuous improvement.
The Committee’s overall assessment of the CMS’ control 
environment is satisfactory and noted that it remained on 
an improving trajectory during the financial year under 
review. The collective commitment of management, 
Council, and other stakeholders to sound governance and 
effective internal controls played a vital role in 
strengthening the overall control environment.
The Committee continued to exercise its oversight 
responsibilities through various mechanisms, including 
monitoring management’s implementation of corrective 
actions, reviewing assurance reports, and engaging with 
the relevant assurance providers. These efforts contributed 
to the continued strengthening and improvement of the 
entity’s overall control environment.
Effectiveness of the Internal Audit Function
The CMS’ Internal Audit Function is outsourced to Lunika 
Inc., which was reappointed for a three-year period from 1 
April 2024 to 31 March 2027 following a competitive 
procurement process.
The ARC reviewed and approved the risk-based Annual 
Internal Audit Plan and monitored its implementation 
through reports presented during the year. Internal Audit 
reported to the Committee on the outcomes of individual 
engagements, findings and recommendations, 
management responses and progress against the 
approved plan.
For the 2025/26 financial year, the Internal Audit Plan 
comprised 11 planned audits, consisting of three 
assurance services, one advisory service and seven 
reviews. The review programme included quarterly 
performance information reviews and probity reviews.
Internal Audit coverage was informed by the CMS’ risk 
profile, management input and other areas approved by 
the ARC. The work undertaken covered governance, risk 
management, internal controls, financial and operational 
information, performance information, ICT, compliance, 
safeguarding of assets and fraud prevention.
91
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
The ARC concluded that the Internal Audit Function was 
effective during the 2025/26 financial year and provided 
appropriate assurance and support to the Committee in 
fulfilling its oversight responsibilities. The Committee 
noted that Internal Audit’s work contributed to the 
effective monitoring of governance, risk management, 
and internal control processes during the period under 
review.
Effectiveness of Risk Management
The ARC monitored the effectiveness of the CMS’ 
enterprise risk management processes and considered 
the organisation’s strategic risk profile and the adequacy 
of measures implemented to mitigate identified risks.
During the year under review, the CMS’ overall strategic 
risk profile improved, with the average residual risk rating 
declining from 14 to 12, placing the organisation within 
the medium-risk category. The Committee notes that this 
improvement reflects strengthened controls and risk 
management practices, while recognising the need for 
continued monitoring of high-impact and emerging risks.
The CMS maintained an approved Enterprise Risk 
Management Policy and Framework, incorporating the 
organisation’s risk appetite and risk tolerance. Risk 
Champions were appointed across business units to 
support the implementation and coordination of risk 
management activities and to promote a risk-aware 
culture across the organisation.
The ARC received ongoing reports on the CMS’ risk 
management processes and strategic risk exposures 
which were duly recommended to Council for 
consideration. Based on the reports presented to it, the 
improvement in the organisation’s risk profile and the 
mitigating measures implemented by management, the 
ARC is satisfied that the CMS maintained an appropriate 
risk management framework and that risk management 
continued to mature during the year under review.
Business Continuity Management
The Committee continued to exercise oversight over the 
Business Continuity Management (BCM) process 
throughout the financial year. The BCM environment 
within the CMS remained acceptable, with opportunities 
identified for further improvement and strengthening.
The CMS maintained Business Continuity Plans and 
Business Impact Analyses, which are reviewed annually or 
when circumstances require. Business continuity 
arrangements were supported by an established Business 
Continuity Management Committee, ICT backup and 
disaster recovery arrangements, and ongoing disaster 
recovery testing.
The CMS had previously undertaken penetration testing 
and vulnerability assessments and, during the year under 
review, commenced preparations for a cyber-risk 
management maturity assessment.
The Committee remained committed to providing 
ongoing oversight of the entity’s BCM arrangements and 
ensuring that appropriate measures are in place to 
enhance organisational resilience, preparedness, and the 
entity’s ability to respond effectively to potential threats 
and disruptions that may impact the continuity of critical 
operations and service delivery.
Adequacy, Reliability and Accuracy of Financial and Per-
formance Information
The ARC reviewed financial and performance information 
presented during the year, including quarterly 
management accounts and performance reports, and 
considered the systems, processes and controls supporting 
the preparation and reporting of this information.
Internal Audit’s approved programme included quarterly 
reviews of performance information, providing the 
Committee with additional assurance over the reliability 
and integrity of reported performance information.
Internal Audit conducted quarterly reviews of the CMS’ 
performance information and concluded that the 
information prepared by management was satisfactory. 
The ARC reviewed the quarterly performance information 
reports, supported by Internal Audit’s review reports, and 
was satisfied that the information presented provided a 
reliable, accurate, and adequate reflection of the entity’s 
performance for each quarter during the 2025/26 financial 
year.
Adequacy and Effectiveness of ICT Governance
The ARC exercised oversight of ICT-related governance, 
risks and controls during the year under review.
The CMS continued to integrate its business continuity, 
ICT backup and disaster recovery arrangements. Disaster 
recovery testing was undertaken to assess operational 
resilience and preparedness for disruptive events. The 
CMS had also undertaken penetration testing and 
vulnerability assessments and commenced preparations 
for a cyber-risk management maturity assessment.
The ICT environment within the CMS continued to improve 
during the year, with the Committee providing ongoing 
oversight over ICT governance, risk management, 
controls, and the implementation of corrective actions. 
Based on the oversight performed, the Committee 
considers the ICT governance environment to be 
satisfactory, adequate, and effective, while recognising 
the need for continued improvement to further strengthen 
ICT governance and support the entity’s operational and 
strategic objectives.
92
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Accounting and Auditing Concerns Identified Through 
Internal and External Audits
The ARC considered accounting and auditing matters 
raised through Internal Audit and the external audit 
process and monitored management’s implementation of 
agreed corrective actions.
The Committee received reports on audit findings, 
management responses and progress in implementing 
corrective measures throughout the reporting period.
Compliance with Legal and Regulatory Provisions
The ARC monitored compliance with applicable legislation 
and regulatory requirements through reports submitted 
by management, Internal Audit and other assurance 
providers.
The Committee considered the adequacy of processes 
established to identify, monitor and address compliance 
obligations and instances of non-compliance.
In-Year Management and Quarterly Reporting
In terms of the PFMA, the Council is required to submit 
quarterly reports to the Executive Authority. These reports 
cover financial management, performance information, 
strategic risk management and compliance.
All quarterly reports were submitted timeously during the 
reporting period as follows:
• Quarter 1 – 31 July 2025
• Quarter 2 – 31 October 2025
• Quarter 3 – 31 January 2026
• Quarter 4 – 30 April 2026
The ARC reviewed the quarterly reports and considered 
the quality and completeness of the financial, performance, 
risk and compliance information presented. No matters of 
concern were raised by the Executive Authority in relation 
to the reports submitted by the Council.
The Committee is satisfied that the CMS complied with its 
quarterly reporting requirements during the reporting 
period.
Combined Assurance
The ARC continued to oversee the development and 
implementation of combined assurance within the CMS 
during the year under review.
The CMS has established a Combined Assurance 
Framework and assurance universe to coordinate 
assurance provided by management, Internal Audit and 
independent and external assurance providers across the 
organisation’s significant risks, business processes, 
systems and controls.
The Committee notes that implementation of the 
combined assurance process remains in progress. The 
ARC will continue to monitor implementation of the 
framework and the development and execution of the 
Combined Assurance Plan to strengthen assurance 
coverage, minimise unnecessary duplication and support 
the effectiveness of the CMS’ control environment.
Evaluation of the Annual Financial Statements
The Committee reviewed the Annual Financial Statements 
(AFS) and, based on its review and the assurance obtained, 
was satisfied that the AFS fairly and accurately represented 
the financial position, financial performance and state of 
affairs of the CMS for the financial year ended 31 March 
2026. In undertaking its review, the Committee considered 
the quality and integrity of the Annual Financial Statements, 
the appropriateness of accounting policies and practices 
and significant financial reporting matters.
In reaching its conclusion, the Committee also relied on 
the work performed by the Internal Audit Function, which 
provided additional assurance and strengthened the 
Committee’s review and assessment of the Annual 
Financial Statements.
External Audit
The external audit of the CMS was conducted by the 
Auditor-General of South Africa. The ARC considered the 
external audit strategy, significant audit risks, audit 
findings and management’s responses to matters raised 
during the audit process.
The Office of the Auditor-General maintained a standing 
invitation to attend the Committee’s meetings throughout 
the year. This engagement strengthens the overall 
assurance framework by providing the Committee with 
independent oversight and insight into the financial 
affairs, governance, risk management, and internal control 
environment of the entity.
The Committee remained available to engage with the 
Office of the Auditor-General throughout the audit 
process and stood ready to provide assistance and 
support where necessary, in line with its oversight 
responsibilities over audit-related matters. The Office of 
the Committee Chairperson maintained regular 
communication with both the Auditor-General and the 
Office of the Chief Financial Officer, supporting effective 
coordination and communication throughout the audit 
process and contributing to the smooth and efficient 
execution of the audit.
Auditor-General’s Report
During the current financial year, the audit findings and 
issues relating to performance information that were 
raised in the previous financial year were successfully 

93
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
addressed and resolved. The Committee relied on the 
work performed by the Internal Audit Function to obtain 
further assurance on the status of these matters. Through 
follow-up audits, Internal Audit independently assessed 
and confirmed the implementation and resolution of both 
internal and external audit findings.
The Post-Audit Implementation Plan proved to be an 
effective monitoring tool used by the Committee to track 
the implementation of agreed corrective actions and to 
ensure that prior-year audit findings were appropriately, 
completely, and accurately resolved. This process 
strengthened the Committee’s oversight of audit matters 
and contributed to improved accountability and the 
sustainability of corrective measures implemented by 
management.
At year-end, the process of addressing historic irregular 
expenditure remained ongoing, with the CMS having 
made substantive progress towards resolving these 
matters. Where instances of wrongdoing were identified, 
the associated consequence management processes 
were also underway. The Loss Control Committee played 
a vital role in facilitating and monitoring these processes, 
while the Committee continued to exercise its oversight 
responsibilities in relation to the appropriate management, 
resolution, and reporting of these matters. 
The Committee is pleased to confirm that, following the 
audit of the entity’s financial statements for the 2025/26 
financial year, the Auditor-General expressed the opinion 
that the financial statements present fairly, in all material 
respects, the financial position of the entity as at 31 March 
2026, as well as its financial performance and cash flows 
for the year then ended, in accordance with the Standards 
of Generally Recognised Accounting Practice (GRAP) and 
the requirements of the Public Finance Management Act 
1 of 1999 (PFMA).
The achievement of a clean audit outcome is testament to 
the CMS’ continued commitment to sound financial 
management, good governance, accountability, and 
effective internal controls. The Committee concurs with 
and accepts the conclusions of the Auditor-General on the 
Annual Financial Statements for the 2025/26 financial 
year.
Accordingly, the Committee is of the opinion that the 
audited Annual Financial Statements should be accepted 
and read together with the Report of the Auditor-General, 
as these collectively provide a fair and comprehensive 
reflection of the entity’s financial position and performance 
for the year under review.
Conclusion
The ARC notes the continued strengthening of the CMS’ 
governance, risk management and assurance environment 
during the year under review. The improvement in the 
organisation’s strategic risk profile and continued oversight 
of internal control, financial and performance reporting, 
risk management and assurance demonstrate progress in 
strengthening organisational governance and 
accountability.
The CMS’ control environment overall was satisfactory 
and remained on an improving trajectory. The Committee, 
Council, management, and various stakeholders remained 
committed to good governance, which was evidenced by 
the entity’s overall improvement in performance and 
achievement of a clean audit outcome. Continuous 
improvement and performance excellence remained key 
priorities for the entity, with a strong focus on ensuring 
that these priorities translated into improved service 
delivery.
The ARC expresses its appreciation to the Council, 
management, Internal Audit, AGSA and other assurance 
providers for their cooperation and contribution during 
the year under review.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Introduction
The Human Resources (HR) sub-programme continued to 
provide strategic support during the reporting period. 
This section presents an overview of the state of human 
resources at the Council for Medical Schemes (CMS) 
during 2025/26. It highlights the progress against the An-
nual Performance Plan through the implementation of key 
HR priorities.
Overview of Human Resources Matters
The Human Resources sub-programme provides strategic 
direction and guidance on human resource policies 
and practices, ensuring compliance with applicable la-
bour legislation. During the reporting period, it support-
ed organisational objectives through: workforce planning; 
performance management; policy development; employ-
ee benefits; training and development; organisational 
development; employment equity; employee relations; 
employee wellness; budget planning, and general HR ad-
ministration.
Workforce Planning Framework & Key  
Strategies to Attract and Recruit a Skilled 
and Capable Workforce
The filling of vacancies during the 2025/26 reporting pe-
riod was accomplished in line with the CMS’ recruitment 
and selection policy and processes. Twelve employees 
were appointed to permanent and fixed-term positions. 
Six positions were filled by external applicants, three 
were filled by internal applicants, and two were filled by 
applicants who participated in work-integrated learning. 
One was filled by an employee who was previously 
appointed on a fixed-term contract. 
The unit continued to implement work-integrated learn-
ing in partnership with higher education and Technical 
and Vocational Education and Training (TVET) institutions, 
providing on-the-job training opportunities to 12 learners 
during the reporting period. In addition, seven temporary 
employees were appointed to support business units in 
meeting performance targets and addressing operational 
backlogs.
Notes: A total of seven terminations were recorded, comprising four resignations for career advancement, one 
non-confirmation of probation, one dismissal, and one retirement.

97
Organisational Development
Organisational Development (OD) focused on enhancing 
organisational effectiveness by aligning structure, people, 
and processes with strategic priorities. OD supported key 
organisational and Information and Communication 
Technology (ICT) initiatives, including providing change 
management support for the M-Files project to enable 
adoption and minimise operational disruption.
OD also strengthened performance management practic-
es in line with policy requirements by facilitating training 
and awareness sessions. Furthermore, it supported the 
alignment of Key Performance Areas (KPAs) to annual 
performance plans and ensured compliance with perfor -
mance contracting processes.
In addition, OD contributed to critical organisational 
initiatives such as risk assessment processes to enhance 
governance and oversight. It also conducted a qualita-
tive assessment of the organisational structure to identify 
alignment, resourcing challenges, and opportunities for 
optimisation.
These interventions collectively improved operational effi-
ciency, enhanced employee capability, and strengthened 
the CMS’ ability to deliver on its strategic and regulatory 
mandate.
Performance Management
Performance management remained a key focus during 
the reporting period. At the start of the 2025/26 financial 
year, the HR sub-programme facilitated the completion 
of annual disclosures of interest and the signing of per -
formance agreements for all employees. These agree-
ments and appraisal documents outlined the outcomes 
and measures aligned to the CMS’ strategic objectives. 
The moderation of the 2024/25 performance assessments 
was finalised on 15 May 2025, and Council approved 
the payment of performance bonuses to bargaining unit 
employees on 25 June 2025.
Employee Wellness Programmes
The CMS remains committed to promoting a healthy and 
supportive work environment, with employee wellness as 
a key priority. An outsourced wellness programme, deliv-
ered by Lyra, provides employees with access to guidance 
and support for achieving a balanced work–life approach. 
HR also introduced a range of wellness initiatives aimed at 
promoting a healthy and productive workforce.
Corporate Social Responsibility
During the reporting period, the CMS’ employees sup-
ported a social responsibility initiative aimed at provid-
ing school shoes to learners at Olivenhoutbosch Pri-
mary School in Centurion. Feedback from educators 
indicated that several learners lacked proper footwear, 
which contributed to instances of absentee-
ism. In response, staff donated more than 50 pairs of 
shoes during the 2025/26 school year, and these were 
distributed to beneficiaries during the winter period.
Policy Development 
Environmental scanning of the existing labour and social 
legislation frameworks was conducted. Signifi-
cant legislative changes and developments impacting our 
policy frameworks were identified with primarily focus 
on the Employment Equity Act, the Basic Conditions of 
Employment Act, and the Labour Relations Act. Coun-
cil approved human resources policies that were found to 
have been impacted by the legislative changes in Febru-
ary 2026.
Employee Benefits
The CMS continued to enhance its employment condi-
tions and, during the period under review, conducted a 
high-level review of its Remuneration Philosophy and Em-
ployment Value Proposition policy. This process will be 
finalised in the next financial year.
Training and Development
During the reporting period, employees participated in a 
range of training initiatives aligned to the approved train-
ing budget and development plans, including academic 
programmes, workshops and seminars. The Workplace 
Skills Plan and Annual Training Report for 2025/26 
were submitted to and approved by  the Health and Wel-
fare Sector Education and Training Authority (HWSETA) on 
15 October 2025, allowing the organisation to continue 
benefiting from mandatory and discretionary grants.
Employment Equity
Compliance with the Employment Equity Act remains a 
key priority for the CMS, with a continued focus on fos-
tering equal employment opportunities across all occupa-
tional levels. A new Employment Equity Plan, covering the 
period 1 September 2025 to 31 August 2030, was devel-
oped to promote equitable representation of designated 
groups. 
Implementation of the plan was monitored by the 
Employment Equity Forum and organised labour during 
the recruitment and selection processes. Progress was 
reported to the Department of Employment and Labour 
during the period under review.
There is a fair representation of designated females at 
Top Management, Senior Management, and Profession-
ally qualified occupational levels with over-representation 
of designated females at Skilled and Semi-skilled occupa-
tional levels when compared to both Provincial EAP and 
Sectoral targets. CMS currently has three people with dis-
abilities as declared in terms of the Employment Equity 
Act. The table that follows provides the snapshot of the 
CMS workforce profile on the 31st of March 2026.
98
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Employee Relations 
One formal disciplinary case was recorded, relating to 
breach and non-compliance with the CMS’ policies, pro-
cedures, National Treasury Regulations, and standard 
operating procedures. Additionally, two interpersonal 
grievances were lodged and effectively addressed.
The HR sub-programme maintained ongoing engagement 
with organised labour on key matters of mutual interest. 
The CMS and the National Education, Health and Allied 
Workers’ Union (NEHAWU) successfully concluded wage 
negotiations following a union dispute and reached 
a settlement agreement on the 2023/24 performance 
bonus matter.  The latter had been arbitrated at the 
Commission for Conciliation, Mediation and Arbitration 
(CCMA) in favour of the union. An employee who did not 
successfully complete probation referred an unfair 
dismissal dispute to the CCMA for conciliation and 
arbitration.
The unfair dismissal dispute referred to the Labour Court 
by a former employee was reviewed following a CCMA 
ruling in favour of the CMS. The Labour Court subsequently 
ruled in favour of the former employee, and the CMS 
resolved to appeal the judgement.
All the CMS’ employees were trained on the New Code of 
Good Practice: Dismissal, which came into effect in 
September 2025.
Achievements
The HR sub-programme achieved the following set of 
priorities during the period under review: 
• Successfully concluded 2025/26 wage negotiations 
without operational disruptions.
• Management and organised labour reached a set-
tlement agreement in respect of the 2023/24 perfor -
mance bonus dispute which was referred to the Labour 
Court.
• Employee wellness programmes were implemented to 
support staff well-being and promote a healthy work–
life balance.
• Timely filling of approved posts to enhance organisa-
tional efficiency.
• Training initiatives were executed in alignment with the 
approved training plan and budget.
• Resource constraints were addressed through the ap-
pointment of contract and experienced employees to 
manage workload backlogs.
Challenges faced by the Entity 
The HR sub-programme encountered several challenges 
during the reporting period:
• Financial constraints resulted in performance bonus 
disputes being referred to the CCMA. 
• Capacity and budget limitations prevented the imple-
mentation of certain key programmes, including the 
filling of approved position and vacancies, succession 
and talent management initiatives, job evaluation and 
salary benchmarking exercises.
• Posts had to be re-advertised due to a shortage of suit-
ably qualified candidates for critical roles. 
Future HR Plans and Goals 
• Assess the effectiveness of the organisational structure.
• Finalisation of the remuneration and incentive policy, 
whose provisions are currently being challenged at the 
CCMA.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Report of the Auditor-General to Parliament on Council for 
Medical Schemes
Report on audit of the financial statements
Opinion
1. I have audited the financial statements of the Council for Medical Schemes set out on pages 117 to 150, which 
comprise the statement of financial position as at 31 March 2026, statement of financial performance, statement 
of changes in net assets, cash flow statement and statement of comparison of budget information with actual in-
formation for the year then ended, as well as notes to the financial statements, including a summary of significant 
accounting policies.
2. In my opinion, the financial statements present fairly, in all material respects, the financial position of the Council 
for Medical Schemes as at 31 March 2026 and its financial performance and cash flows for the year then ended 
in accordance with the Generally Recognised Accounting Practice (GRAP) and the requirements of the Public Fi-
nance Management Act 1 of 1999 (PFMA).
Basis for opinion
3. I conducted my audit in accordance with the International Standards on Auditing (ISAs). My responsibilities un-
der those standards are further described in the responsibilities of the Auditor-General for the audit of financial 
statements section of my report.
4. I am independent of the public entity in accordance with the International Ethics Standards Board for Accoun-
tants’ International Code of Ethics for Professional Accountants (including International Independence Standards) 
(IESBA code) as well as other ethical requirements that are relevant to my audit in South Africa. I have fulfilled my 
other ethical responsibilities in accordance with these requirements and the IESBA code.
5. I believe the that audit evidence I have obtained is sufficient and appropriate to provide a basis for my opinion.
Responsibilities of the Accounting Authority
6. The Accounting Authority is responsible for the preparation and fair presentation of the financial statements in 
accordance with the GRAP and the requirements of the PFMA; and for such internal control as the accounting 
authority determines is necessary to enable the preparation of financial statements that are free from material 
misstatement, whether due to fraud or error.
7. In preparing the financial statements, the accounting authority is responsible for assessing the entity’s ability to 
continue as a going concern; disclosing, as applicable, matters relating to going concern; and using the going 
concern basis of accounting unless the appropriate governance structure either intends to liquidate the entity or 
to cease operations or has no realistic alternative to do so.
Responsibilities of the Auditor-General for the audit of the financial statements
8. My objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements as a whole are free from 
material misstatement, whether due to fraud or error; and to issue an Auditor’s Report that includes my opinion. 
Reasonable assurance is a high level of assurance but is not a guarantee that an audit conducted in accordance 
with the ISAs will always detect a material misstatement when it exists. Misstatements can arise from fraud or error 
and are considered material if, individually or in aggregate, they could reasonably be expected to influence the 
economic decisions of users taken on the basis of these financial statements.
9. A further description of my responsibilities for the audit of the financial statements is included in the annexure to 
this Auditor’s Report. This description, which is located at page 114 of the annexure forms part of my Auditor’s 
Report.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Report on the Annual Performance Report
10. In accordance with the Public Audit Act 25 of 2004 (PAA) and the general notice issued in terms thereof; I must 
audit and report on the usefulness and reliability of the reported performance information against predetermined 
objectives for the selected material performance indicators presented in the annual performance report. The ac-
counting authority is responsible for the preparation of the annual performance report.
11. I selected the following material performance indicators related to Member Protection presented in the annual 
performance report for the year ended 31 March 2026. I selected those indicators that measure the public entity’s 
performance on its primary mandated functions and that are of significant national, community or public interest.
• Percentage of Customer Care interventions resulting from calls, e-mailed queries and walk-in consultations 
handled by the customer care centre.
• Percentage of complaints older than 120 calendar days adjudicated during the reporting period in accordance 
with the complaints standard operating procedures.
• Percentage of category 2 complaints adjudicated within 120 calendar days and in accordance with the com-
plaints standard operating procedures.
• Percentage of category 1 complaints adjudicated within 60 calendar days and in accordance with the com-
plaints standard operating procedures.
• The number of CMScripts published.
• Percentage of category 1 clinical opinions provided within 30 working days of receipt of a request from the 
complaints adjudication sub-programme.
• Percentage of category 2 clinical opinions provided within 60 working days of receipt of a request from the 
complaints adjudication sub-programme.
•  Percentage of category 3 clinical opinions provided within 90 working days of receipt of a request from the 
complaints adjudication sub-programme.
12. I evaluated the reported performance information for the selected material performance indicators against the 
criteria developed from the performance management and reporting framework, as defined in the general notice. 
When an annual performance report is prepared using these criteria, it provides useful and reliable information and 
insights to users on the public entity’s planning and delivery on its mandate and objectives.
13. I performed procedures to test whether:
• The indicators used for planning and reporting on performance can be linked directly to the public entity’s 
mandate and the achievement of its planned objectives.
• All the indicators relevant for measuring the public entity’s performance against its primary mandated and pri-
oritised functions and planned objectives are included.
• The indicators are well defined to ensure that they are easy to understand and can be applied consistently, 
as well as verifiable so that I can confirm the methods and processes to be used for measuring achievements.
• The targets can be linked directly to the achievement of the indicators and are specific, time bound and mea-
surable to ensure that it is easy to understand what should be delivered and by when, the required level of 
performance as well as how performance will be evaluated.
• The indicators and targets reported on in the annual performance report are the same as those committed to 
in the approved initial or revised planning documents.
• The reported performance information is presented in the annual performance report in the prescribed man-
ner.
• There is adequate supporting evidence for the achievements reported and for the reasons provided for any 
over- or underachievement of targets.
14. I performed the procedures for the purpose of reporting material findings only; and not to express an assurance 
opinion or conclusion.
15. I did not identify any material findings on the reported performance information for the selected material indica-
tors.

Report on compliance with legislation

counting authority is responsible for the public entity’s compliance with legislation.

AGSA findings engagement methodology. This engagement is not an assurance engagement. Accordingly, I do 
not express an assurance opinion or conclusion.
21. Through an established AGSA process, I selected requirements in key legislation for compliance testing that are 
relevant to the financial and performance management of the public entity, clear to allow consistent measurement 
and evaluation, while also sufficiently detailed and readily available to report in an understandable manner. The 
selected legislative requirements are included in the annexure to this Auditor’s Report.
22. I did not identify material non-compliance with the selected legislative requirements.
Other information in the annual report
23. The accounting authority is responsible for the other information included in the annual report which includes the 
chairperson’s foreword, chief executive officer’s overview, audit committee’s report and human resources manage-
ment. The other information referred to does not include the financial statements, the Auditor’s Report and those 
selected material indicators in the scoped-in programme presented in the annual performance report that have 
been specifically reported on in this Auditor’s Report.
24. My opinion on the financial statements and my reports on the audit of the annual performance report and com-
pliance with legislation do not cover the other information included in the annual report and I do not express an 
audit opinion or any form of assurance conclusion on it.
25. My responsibility is to read this other information and, in doing so, consider whether it is materially inconsistent 
with the financial statements and the selected material indicators in the scoped-in programme presented in the an-
nual performance report or my knowledge obtained in the audit, or otherwise appears to be materially misstated.

Auditor-General’s responsibility for the audit
Professional judgement and professional scepticism
As part of an audit in accordance with the ISAs, I exercise professional judgement and maintain professional scepti-
cism throughout my audit of the financial statements and the procedures performed on reported performance informa-
tion for selected material performance indicators and on the public entity’s compliance with selected requirements 
in key legislation.
Financial statements
In addition to my responsibility for the audit of the financial statements as described in this Auditor’s Report, I also:
• Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of the financial statements, whether due to fraud or error; 
design and perform audit procedures responsive to those risks; and obtain audit evidence that is sufficient and 
appropriate to provide a basis for my opinion. The risk of not detecting a material misstatement resulting from 
fraud is higher than for one resulting from error, as fraud may involve collusion, forgery, intentional omissions, 
misrepresentations, or the override of internal control.
• Obtain an understanding of internal control relevant to the audit in order to design audit procedures that are 
appropriate in the circumstances, but not for the purpose of expressing an opinion on the effectiveness of the 
public entity’s internal control.
• Evaluate the appropriateness of accounting policies used and the reasonableness of accounting estimates and 
related disclosures made.
• Conclude on the appropriateness of the use of the going concern basis of accounting in the preparation of the 
financial statements. I also conclude, based on the audit evidence obtained, whether a material uncertainty 
exists relating to events or conditions that may cast significant doubt on the ability of the public entity to con-
tinue as a going concern. If I conclude that a material uncertainty exists, I am required to draw attention in my 
Auditor’s Report to the related disclosures in the financial statements about the material uncertainty or, if such 
disclosures are inadequate, to modify my opinion on the financial statements. My conclusions are based on 
the information available to me at the date of this Auditor’s Report. However, future events or conditions may 
cause a public entity to cease operating as a going concern.
• Evaluate the overall presentation, structure and content of the financial statements, including the disclosures, 
and determine whether the financial statements represent the underlying transactions and events in a manner 
that achieves fair presentation.
Communication with those charged with governance
I communicate with the accounting authority regarding, among other matters, the planned scope and timing of the au-
dit and significant audit findings, including any significant deficiencies in internal control that I identify during my audit.
I also provide the accounting authority with a statement that I have complied with relevant ethical requirements regard-
ing independence and communicate with them all relationships and other matters that may reasonably be thought to 
bear on my independence and, where applicable, actions taken to eliminate threats or safeguards applied.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
The Council members are required by the PFMA (Act 1 
of 1999), to maintain adequate accounting records and 
are responsible for the content and integrity of the Annu-
al Financial Statements and related financial information 
included in this report. It is the responsibility of the 
members to ensure that the Annual Financial Statements 
fairly present the state of affairs of the entity as at the end 
of the financial year and the results of its operations and 
cash flows for the period then ended. The external 
auditors are engaged to express an independent 
opinion on the Annual Financial Statements and were 
given unrestricted access to all financial records and re-
lated data.
The Annual Financial Statements have been prepared in 
accordance with Standards of Generally Recognised Ac-
counting Practice (GRAP) including any interpretations, 
guidelines and directives issued by the Accounting Stan-
dards Board.
The Annual Financial Statements are based upon appropri-
ate accounting policies consistently applied and support-
ed by reasonable and prudent judgements and estimates.
The Council members acknowledge that they are ultimately 
responsible for the system of internal financial control 
established by the entity and place considerable 
importance on maintaining a strong control environment. 
To enable the members to meet these responsibilities, the 
members set standards for internal control aimed at 
reducing the risk of error or deficit in a cost-effective 
manner. The standards include the proper delegation of 
responsibilities within a clearly defined framework, 
effective accounting procedures and adequate segrega-
tion of duties to ensure an acceptable level of risk. 
These controls are monitored throughout the entity 
and all employees are required to maintain the highest 
ethical standards in ensuring the entity’s business is con-
ducted in a manner that in all reasonable circumstances 
is above reproach. The focus of risk management in the 
entity is on identifying, assessing, managing and monitor-
ing all known forms of risk across the entity. 
While operating risk cannot be fully eliminated, the entity 
endeavours to minimise it by ensuring that appropriate 
infrastructure, controls, systems and ethical behaviour 
are applied and managed within predetermined proce-
dures and constraints.
The Council members are of the opinion, based on the 
information and explanations given by management, that 
the system of internal control provides reasonable 
assurance that the financial records may be relied on for 
the preparation of the Annual Financial Statements. 
However, any system of internal financial control can 
provide only reasonable, and not absolute, assurance 
against material misstatement or deficit.
The Council members have reviewed the entity’s cash 
flow forecast for the year to 31 March 2027 and, in the light 
of this review and the current financial position, they are 
satisfied that the entity has or has access to adequate 
resources to continue in operational existence for 
the foreseeable future even though the net assets position 
has decreased.
The Annual Financial Statements are prepared on the ba-
sis that the entity is a going concern and that the entity 
has neither the intention nor the need to liquidate or 
curtail materially the scale of the entity.
Although the Council members are primarily responsible 
for the financial affairs of the entity, they are supported by

The external auditors are responsible for independently

cial Statements. The Annual Financial Statements have

Basis of accounting
The approved budget is on cash basis, thus recognising 
transactions and other events only when cash is received 
or paid. The actual amounts were based on the accrual 
basis of accounting and were adjusted to be comparable 
to the budget which is on cash basis.
Classification basis
The classification basis adopted in the approved budget 
is according to the economic classification as per the Na-
tional Treasury ENE database.
Period of the approved budget
01 April 2025 - 31 March 2026
Approval of the levy rate
The 2025/26 levy rate was approved in terms of section 
2(4) of the Council for Medical Schemes Levies Act (58 of 
2000) by the Minister of Health with concurrence of the 
Finance Minister on 3 June 2025. Calculated materiali-
ty significance value as determined in terms of Treasury 
regulation 28.3.1 amounts to R2 269 000. Positive and 
negative differences above the calculated materiality are 
explained below:
1. Surplus funds: CMS obtained approval to retain sur-
plus funds relating to the 2024/25 financial year. The 
difference is due to timing as CMS already had the 
cash in the bank account.
2. Personnel: Additional performance bonuses relat-
ing to the 2023/24 financial year were paid during 
2025/26 following a CCMA award. The CMS also 
filled key positions which were vacant in 2024/25.
3. Legal fees: The over-expenditure was driven by an 
increase in complex legal matters spanning several 
years, high-conflict disputes progressing to the High 
Court, and the need for specialised legal expertise, 
including senior counsel.
4. Other unclassified expenditure: This line item in-
cludes goods and services not individually disclosed. 
The main cost driver that led to the over-expenditure 
is the appointment of temporary workers and interns 
to relieve operational pressures.
5. Computer services: The over-expenditure is mainly 
due to additional Microsoft Azure licences which were 
not budgeted for.
