CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Compliance and Investigations
The CMS has a focus on enforcing and encouraging 
compliance to ensure the industry’s health and quality of 
services. As such, the following section will address the 
issues of routine inspections, annual general meetings, 
enforcement actions, curatorship and regulatory 
cooperation.
Routine Inspections
The Compliance and Investigations Unit continued 
to strengthen regulatory oversight through routine inspec-
tions conducted on four medical schemes selected using 
a risk-based approach during the year under review. 
These inspections provide an opportunity to assess 
compliance with the Medical Schemes Act and applica-
ble regulatory requirements, identify emerging risks 
and strengthen governance and compliance manage-
ment within medical schemes.
Beyond assessing compliance, the inspections have 
become an important mechanism for promoting continu-
ous improvement. Feedback received from schemes in-
spected during both the current and previous reporting 
periods indicates that the inspections have contributed 
positively to enhancing internal compliance management 
processes. It also strengthened governance practices 
and improved overall compliance risk profiles.
Medical schemes also expressed appreciation for the 
CMS’ collaborative regulatory approach and its continued 
willingness to engage on compliance matters. These 
engagements provide schemes with regulatory guidance 
and clarity while respecting the independence of scheme 
governance structures and decision-making processes.
Annual General Meetings
During the year under review, the unit observed 44 medi-
cal scheme meetings, including Annual General Meetings 
(AGM) and Special General Meetings  (SGM), to 
monitor governance practices and compliance with leg-
islative and regulatory requirements.
These engagements enabled the CMS to assess 
governance processes, review key decisions, and identify 
emerging regulatory or compliance concerns. Meeting 
reports were prepared following each engagement, 
documenting attendance, proceedings, and significant 
matters discussed.
The CMS observed that most schemes continued 
to provide comprehensive AGM documentation. 
They also presented members with detailed reports 
on operational performance, financial statements, gov-
ernance matters, and strategic risks. These engagements 
remain an important source of regulatory intelligence, 
enabling the CMS to identify potential compliance 
issues and engage proactively with schemes where nec-
essary.
Regulatory feedback and guidance arising from these 
observations were shared with Principal Officers and 
Boards of Trustees through the Principal Officer and 
Trustee Forums held during the year. These engagements 
continue to strengthen governance, promote regulatory 
compliance, and encourage a culture of continuous 
improvement across the medical schemes industry.
Enforcement Actions
The CMS continued to undertake enforcement actions 
through Section 43 enquiries to promote compliance with 
the Medical Schemes Act and protect the interests of 
medical scheme beneficiaries. Where appropriate, the 
outcomes of these enquiries resulted in further regulatory 
interventions, including commissioned inspections and 
other enforcement measures aimed at addressing 
identified areas of non-compliance.
Curatorship
The CMS continued to use statutory governance 
interventions where necessary to safeguard the interests of 
beneficiaries and ensure the sound administration of 
medical schemes.
On 4 September 2025, the CMS placed Sizwe-Hosmed 
Medical Scheme under curatorship to protect the interests 
of members and restore sound governance and 
administration within the scheme.
Regulatory Cooperation
As part of its commitment to strengthening regulatory 
cooperation within the region, the CMS hosted the 
Namibia Financial Institutions Supervisory Authority 
(NAMFISA) during the year under review. The engagement 
supported NAMFISA’s implementation of its Market 
Conduct Supervisory Framework by sharing the CMS’ 
regulatory experience, supervisory approaches, and 
practical lessons in overseeing the medical schemes 
environment.
The engagement further strengthened institutional 
relationships and demonstrated the CMS’ role as 
a respected regulator contributing to the development 
of regulatory capacity across the Southern African region.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Demarcation Regulations
No substantive policy changes occurred during the 
year under review regarding the exemption frame-
work for insurance products conducting the business 
of a medical scheme. The exemption period, extended 
through Circular 9 of 2025, is in effect until 31 March 
2027. The extension continues to provide regu-
latory certainty while the government finalises 
its position on the Low-Cost Benefit Option (LCBO) 
framework.
The CMS continues to engage with the Nation-
al Department of Health, National Treasury, and 
other stakeholders regarding the future of the LCBO 
framework. The outcome of these engagements, to-
gether with any direction provided by the Minister of 
Health, will determine whether the current exemption 
requires a further extension beyond March 2027, or 
whether a final policy framework will be implemented.
The following court rulings prevailed with regard to civ-
il proceedings between the CMS and certain healthcare 
providers:
Discovery Health (Pty) Ltd and Netcare 
Plus (Pty) Ltd vs the Registrar of Medical 
Schemes (Appeals Committee)
In the case between Discovery Health and Netcare Plus vs 
Registrar of Medical Schemes (Appeal Committee) the 
following decision was issued: The Appeals Committee 
was required to determine whether voucher-based 
healthcare products offered by Netcare Plus and Discovery 
Health constituted the business of a medical scheme in 
contravention of section 20(1) of the MSA. Both appeals 
were dismissed, with the Appeals Committee confirming 
the Registrar’s decision that the voucher arrangements 
amounted to the unlawful operation of a medical scheme.
The ruling affirms the CMS’ jurisdiction over non-tradition-
al healthcare products that mimic medical scheme bene-
fits. It reinforces the principle that form does not override 
substance, and that products structured as vouchers may 
still fall within the statutory definition of a medical scheme. 
It provides regulatory clarity on the limits of product 
innovation within the healthcare funding environment. The 
Registrar’s powers under section 45 of the MSA were 
upheld, confirming the authority to investigate suspected 
unregistered scheme activity and to require disclosure of 
relevant information. Challenges relating to alleged 
procedural unfairness and bias were dismissed. 
The Appeals Committee confirmed that the Registrar 
acted within statutory powers and the administrative 
process followed was lawful and procedurally sound.
Epion Healthcare Solutions (Pty) Ltd vs CMS 
in the High Court
In the case between Epion Healthcare Solutions (Pty) Ltd 
vs CMS, the applicant sought to review the CMS’ 
decisions under the Promotion of Administrative 
Justice Act (PAJA), alleging: The High Court found that 
the applicant failed to establish a case for review, and 
accordingly upheld the CMS’ decision. The judgment 
confirms the robustness of the CMS’ decision making 
processes under PAJA. It reinforces that healthcare models 
(including direct primary-care offerings) cannot be lawfully 
provided without appropriate exemption under sec-
tion 8(h) of the MSA. It affirms the importance of 
evidence-based and legally grounded regulatory 
determinations. This matter strengthens the CMS’ 
regulatory stance against attempts to bypass statutory 
compliance through alternative healthcare funding 
mechanisms.
Sizwe-Hosmed Curatorship
During the reporting period, the curatorship of the Sizwe-
Hosmed Medical Scheme remained a key regulatory 
intervention aimed at stabilising governance, improving 
financial management, and safeguarding member 
interests.
The curatorship process continued to focus on:
• Strengthening governance and oversight structures 
within the Scheme.
• Addressing historical operational and financial defi-
ciencies.
• Enhancing compliance with the Medical Schemes Act 
(131 of 1998) and regulatory directives.
The CMS maintained close supervision of the curator’s 
activities to ensure alignment with the objectives of 
curatorship, namely the restoration of the Scheme to a 
position of sustainability, accountability, and effective 
administration.
The intervention underscores the CMS’ commitment to 
proactive regulatory action where schemes experience 
governance and financial distress. This ensures that 
member benefits are protected and that confidence in the 
medical scheme’s environment is upheld.
The curatorship remains ongoing, with continued 
monitoring and periodic reporting to assess progress 
against the recovery plan and determine the appropriate 
pathway toward eventual normalisation of the Scheme’s 
operations.

Benefits Management
Contributions increased across the industry in 2026. The section below will give a detailed explanation of the 
increases related to beneficiaries as well as families. It will also focus on increases relative to consumer inflation.
Beneficiary Contribution Increases for 2026
As far as beneficiary contribution increases are concerned, beneficiary contributions for 2026 in terms of the weighted 
average rand amount and increases are split in terms of gross contributions and risk-only contributions. By 
definition, gross contributions are those that include Member Savings Accounts, while risk contributions are those 
that are related to pooled contributions of the scheme with no Member Savings Accounts.
As in Table 60 below, the 2026 total scheme weighted average gross contributions rose by 6.8% to R2 839, with an 
open scheme average of R3 146 (a 5.7% increase) and a restricted scheme average of R2 434 (a 9.7% increase). At 
a beneficiary type level, 2026 rates reached R3 759  for main members, R3 208 for adult dependants, and R1 356 
for child dependants, representing respective increases of 5.6%, 6.9%, and 10.9% from the previous year. Restricted 
schemes saw the highest individual percentage surges in gross costs, with child contributions spiking by 15.5% to R1 
310, and adult contributions increasing by 11.6% to R2 798.
Risk contributions illustrate a similar upward trajectory with contribution increases in 2026 as seen in Table 60 above, 
with the total scheme weighted average climbing by 7.1% to reach R2 567 in 2026. 
This increase consists of an open scheme average of R2 780 (up 5.6%) and a restricted scheme average of R2 286, 
which marks a significant 10.3% jump. At a beneficiary type level, the total 2026 risk contribution figures stand at 
R3 394 for main members (a 5.8% increase), R2 894 for adult dependants (a 7.5% increase), and R1 237 for 
children (an 11.5% increase). Across both financial sectors, restricted schemes consistently experienced the sharpest 
year-over-year percentage hikes, particularly noted in the risk contributions for children under restricted schemes, 
which went up by 16.5% to reach R1 231.
Average Family Contribution Increases for 2026
Contributions by the average family also show an important increase. The average family composition alongside corre-
sponding monthly medical scheme contributions from 2025 to 2026 is summarised in Table 61. The average family size 
saw minor shifts in dependants, with adult dependants increasing slightly from 0.448169 in 2025 to 0.449744 in 2026, 
and child dependants marginally declining from 0.747242 to 0.739283 over the same period.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
In terms of the financial impact on an average size family, the average monthly gross contributions for a family unit 
rose by 6.7%, climbing from R5 817.16 in 2025 to R6 204.78 in 2026. Concurrently, the average risk contributions followed 
a similar upward trajectory, registering a 7.0% increase as costs went up from R5 243.90 to R5 610.30 per month.
Medical Scheme Contribution Increases Relative to Consumer Inflation
An important consideration with regard to medical scheme contribution increases is the notion of consumer inflation. 
The graph below (Figure 12) illustrates the trends of the Average Consumer Price Index (CPI) (sourced from Stats 
SA) and the Average Medical Scheme Contribution Increases. Historically, medical contribution increases 
outpaced general inflation. However, the trend briefly reversed during the pandemic recovery period between 
2021 and 2022, where medical scheme increases lagged significantly behind consumer inflation, dropping to a 
negative difference of -3.3% in 2022.
From 2023 as seen in Figure 12, medical scheme increases have consistently outpaced the CPI. This gap peaked 
dramatically in 2025 at 6.9%, driven by a substantial 10.1% medical contribution increase against a 3.2% CPI. 
For 2026, gross contribution projections indicate a continuing but slightly narrowed disparity, with an expected medical 
contribution increase of 6.8% and a projected CPI of 3.4%, sustaining a 3.4% premium over general economic inflation.
Conclusion
To conclude, the result of medical scheme contribu-
tion increases in relation to consumer inflation raises con-
cerns. The widening differentials between medical 
scheme contribution increases and general economic 
inflation, as evidenced by the historical data in Figure 12 
places financial pressure on existing medical scheme ben-
eficiaries. When medical contribution increases continue 
to outpace the Consumer Price Index (CPI) – reaching a 
peak difference of 6.9% in 2025 and a projected 3.4% gap 
in 2026 – household budgets are forced to absorb health-
care costs that rise at a higher rate than typical salary 
growth. 
This financial strain is further compounded by the sub-
stantial year-on-year spikes in the family-level costs shown 
in Table 61. Over time, this compounding deficit forc-
es existing members to make difficult trade-offs, such as 
downgrading their  benefit options or entirely leaving the 
medical scheme.
Persistently above-inflationary contribution increases also 
serve as a barrier to entry for prospective, younger, or 
lower-income members looking to access private 
healthcare. This barrier creates an adverse selection cycle 
for the medical schemes themselves; if younger, healthier 
individuals are priced out of the market, the membership 
pool becomes older and sicker, driving claims higher and 
inevitably triggering further aggressive contribution 
increases.
The trend demonstrates an urgent need for an innovative 
solution in the market. The purpose will be to arrest the 
spiralling annual medical scheme contribution rate 
increases. In this way beneficiaries will be insulated from 
the risk of losing their cover due to affordability constraints 
and therefore the accessibility gap for the broader 
population will be narrowed.

CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Complaints Adjudication Trends
The CMS continued to investigate and adjudicate complaints in accordance with its mandate to protect the interests 
of medical scheme beneficiaries.
During the period under review, the Complaints Adjudication sub-division played an important role by providing ad-
vice to medical scheme members in respect of their benefits, rights, and obligations. It also provided general advice on 
the business of a medical scheme. Where members were unable to satisfactorily resolve their disputes at scheme level, 
the CMS provided an opportunity for the submission of complaints in terms of Section 47 of the Medical Schemes Act. 
Processing of Written Enquiries and Complaints
The Complaints Adjudication sub-division is the primary processing hub for written enquiries and complaints submit-
ted by medical scheme beneficiaries and other aggrieved stakeholders. The sub-division is responsible for end-to-end 
complaints processing and adjudication. It assists medical scheme members with written guidance in relation to the 
Medical Schemes Act and provides advice on the correct application and interpretation of the medical scheme rules. 
In total, 10 818 emails were processed in the main complaints mailbox and from these, 8 363 enquiries were responded 
to, and 2 455 complaints were formally registered. The 8 363 enquiries were split between Out-of-jurisdiction, prema-
ture complaints and general queries.

Member Protection Overview
The protection of medical scheme members is central to the mandate of the CMS. During the 2025/26 financial year, 
the CMS continued to strengthen member protection through an integrated approach encompassing clinical exper -
tise, complaints adjudication, member education and customer care. These functions work together to support mem-
bers in understanding and accessing their entitled benefits, resolving disputes and navigating the medical schemes 
environment.

Additional Correspondence Processed
In addition to new enquiries and complaints, the subdivision processed a significant volume of related written 
correspondence, including:
• Approximately 5 800 follow-up queries on pending complaints.
• 3 593 complaint responses, including supplementary responses.
• 147 appeals and related correspondence.
• 329 emails relating to health insurance complaints and demarcation product queries.
Registration of Complaints
An analysis of new complaints registered during the 2025/26 financial year revealed a 25.1% year-on-year increase, 
with 493 more complaints registered when compared with the previous financial year. The increase was evident across 
most of the reporting period, with higher volumes recorded in 10 of the 12 months, signalling sustained pressure 
on complaint registration. February 2026 reflected the sharpest increase with 104 additional complaints and a 140% 
year-on-year spike. The fourth quarter of the 2025/26 financial year recorded the highest quarterly volume at 662 
complaints.
165
CMS ANNUAL REPORT 2025/26
Together with 456 complaints carried forward from the previous period, the total number of complaints investigated 
in 2025/26 reached 2 911. These carried-forward matters did not constitute a backlog; rather, they were current com-
plaints that remained within the applicable investigation timelines at the start of the financial year.
Complaints Lodged by Entity Type
During the 2025/26 financial year, 2 455 new complaints were lodged against medical schemes, administrators, and 
health insurance providers. These complaints were itemised as follows:
• Medical schemes: 2 413 new complaints
• Health insurance providers: 23 complaints
• Administrators: 19 complaints
Of the 2 455 new complaints lodged during the 2025/26 reporting period, 2 323 (94.6%) were assessed as justiciable 
at the registration stage, while the remaining 132 (5.4%) were deemed non-justiciable.
Medical scheme complaints accounted for 98.3% of all complaints lodged during the reporting period, at 2 413. The 
remaining 1.7% (42) was made up of health insurance product complaints at 0.9% and administrator complaints at 
0.8%, respectively.
Increase in Complaint Volumes
The 2025/26 financial year saw a notable increase in complaint volumes, with medical schemes complaints going up 
24.6% from the 1 937 complaints reported in 2024/25 to 2 413 in the current reporting period. 
Complaints against other regulated entities also rose sharply from only 25 complaints reported in 2024/25 to 42 regis-
tered in 2025/26, representing a 68% increase. 
Analysis of Medical Scheme Complaints
The total number of medical scheme complaints received during the reporting period was 2 413, of which 1 774 were 
lodged against open medical schemes and 639 were against restricted medical schemes. 
Open medical scheme complaints rose by 21.6% from 1 459 in 2024/25 to 1 774 in the period under review. Similar -
ly, complaints against restricted medical schemes also increased from 478 in 2024/25 to 639 in 2025/26, reflecting a 
33.7% rise. 
Analysis of Complaints Submitted Against Other Regulated Entities
Health insurance product complaints increased by 35.3% from 17 to 23, while Administrator complaints increased by 
171.4%, from 7 in 2024/25 to 19 in 2025/26. The increase in administrator complaints followed the release of the final 
Section 59 Investigation Report during the second quarter of 2025/26. 
Increase in Volumes
The years of 2025/26 saw a notable increase in complaint volumes, with medical schemes 
complaints going up 24.6% from the 1937 complaints reported in 2024/25 to 2413 in the 
current reporting period. 
Complaints against other regulated entities also rose sharply 
from only 25 complaints reported in 2024/25 to 42 registered in 2025/26, 
representing a 68% increase.

Customer Care Centre Trends
The Customer Care Unit is committed to providing optimal communication with consumers. It maintained consistent 
performance across all communication channels, supported by improved system utilisation and enhanced reporting 
capabilities. 
In support of CMS’ statutory mandate under Section 7 of the Medical Schemes Act (131 of 1998), the unit continues 
to serve as a key access point for beneficiaries and stakeholders.  As such, it provides regulatory guidance, 
complaints support, and medical scheme-related information.
Service Volumes and Performance
During the period under review, the Customer Care Unit recorded the following service volumes:
• 25 829 inbound calls, of which 22 438 were successfully handled, reflecting an annual handling rate of 86.87%
• 5 375 email queries responded to
• 95 walk-in consultations attended to 
The results reflect improved operational consistency and strengthened service delivery processes, supported by the 
stabilisation of the automated call logging system introduced during the previous financial year.
Comparative Performance Trends (2024/25 vs 2025/26)
A comparative analysis between the two financial years highlights the following key trends:
• Inbound calls decreased from 27 896 (2024/25) to 25 829 (2025/26), indicating improved channel efficiency and a 
possible migration of queries to alternative platforms. 
• Call-handling performance improved, with the 2025/26 financial year achieving a measurable 86.87% handling rate, 
reflecting enhanced system reliability and operational discipline. 
• Email volumes declined significantly from 10 860 to 5 375, while prior analysis indicated a high proportion of 
non-service-related correspondence during the previous year. 
• Walk-in consultations increased marginally from 90 to 95, demonstrating stable demand for in-person assistance. 
Overall, the Customer Care Centre demonstrates improved measurement accuracy, enhanced system-supported re-
porting, and a more structured service environment.

Key Observations
The following key observations were made: 
• The Customer Care Unit continues to demonstrate operational resilience and improved service consistency despite 
continued demand for accessible regulatory and consumer support.
• The 86.87% call handling rate reflects strengthened system utilisation and improved workflow management.
• The introduction and stabilisation of the automated call logging system has significantly enhanced data integrity and 
performance visibility. 
• The reduction in email volumes suggests improved stakeholder routing and potential channel optimisation. 
• Broker accreditation remains the dominant query driver, accounting for the highest proportion of stakeholder en-
gagement. 
• The distribution of queries highlights the CMS’ dual role in regulatory interpretation support and consumer protec-
tion services.
The 2025/26 financial year reflects a Customer Care Centre that is increasingly stable, data-driven, and system-support-
ed. While service volumes show variation compared to the previous year, the unit has achieved stronger perfor-
mance outcomes, improved handling efficiency, and more reliable reporting structures.

Clinical Consulting Services provides the clinical evidence, 
expertise and regulatory interpretation that enables the 
CMS to fulfil its statutory mandate to protect beneficia-
ries, promote appropriate and equitable access to enti-
tled benefits, support consistent regulation of medical 
schemes, and contribute to quality and outcomes in the 
private healthcare system.
During the 2025/26 financial year, the Clinical Unit con-
tinued to advance member empowerment by providing 
clinical guidance, education, and technical support. The 
Unit published 12 CMScripts addressing a range of key 
health topics to improve stakeholder understanding of 
healthcare benefits and PMBs. 
The Unit provided 602 clinical opinions to support regula-
tory decision-making and stakeholder enquiries. Of these, 
451 were Category 1 opinions issued within 30 working 
days of receipt, 92 were Category 2 opinions issued within 
60 working days, and 23 were Category 3 opinions issued 
within 90 working days. A further 36 opinions were issued 
outside the applicable timeframes. In addition, the Unit 
managed approximately 1 013 clinical enquiries, prioritis-
ing urgent and complex cases to ensure timely guidance 
and support.
The Clinical Unit also played an active role in policy de-
velopment. It contributed technical and clinical expertise 
to the Benefit Definition Guidelines and ongoing PMB 
Review processes. It also provided close collaboration 
with, and support for, research activities, the CMS Indus-
try Indaba, and the upcoming launch of the CMS research 
journal. 
The Unit furthermore collaborated closely with Education 
and Training, delivering PMB-related training and educa-
tional initiatives for a range of stakeholders. 
There has also been close collaboration with the Stake-
holder Relations Unit, contributing to the increased CMS 
visibility and engagement with external stakeholders, in-
cluding clinical academic institutions. The Bua magazine, 
a health communications magazine, was launched by the 
Member Protection Unit in collaboration with Stakehold-
er Relations. The objective of the Bua magazine is to in-
crease member understanding of their health cover, PMBs 
and the health systems, to support efficient and meaning-
ful use of their health funding.
With regard to external stakeholders, the collaboration 
remained strong throughout the year. This fostered con-
structive engagement and supported the Council’s broad-
er objectives of protecting beneficiaries, promoting ac-
cess to quality healthcare, and strengthening the effective 
implementation of PMB provisions.
Clinical Consulting Services
Education and Training
The Education and Training Unit has an ongoing commit-
ment to strengthening knowledge, governance, and com-
pliance within the medical schemes industry.  As such, it 
successfully delivered a total of 76 training, education, 
empowerment, and awareness initiatives during the year 
under review. These interventions targeted a broad range 
of stakeholders, including medical scheme beneficiaries, 
Boards of Trustees, brokers, medical schemes, service pro-
viders, administrators, and organisations affiliated with 
the Consumer Protection Forum (CPF).
The Unit enhanced public awareness of the role and man-
date of the CMS, as well as the rights, responsibilities, and 
obligations of medical scheme members. As such,  the 
Unit conducted 59 consumer empowerment sessions 
across eight of the country’s nine provinces. It contributed 
significantly to increased stakeholder engagement and 
the visibility of the CMS at community level.
The Unit further delivered a range of organisation-specific 
and scheme-specific customised training programmes 
tailored to the unique needs of stakeholders. A notable 
achievement during the reporting period was the success-
ful implementation of the Advanced Trustee Leadership 
Programme in partnership with the Gordon Institute of 
Business Science (GIBS). The programme, pitched  at 
NQF 8 level standard, is designed to strengthen strategic 
leadership and governance capabilities among trustees. 
Thirty-seven delegates representing open and restrict-
ed medical schemes and one administrator successfully 
completed the programme and received certification.
In support of continuous professional development within 
the industry, the Unit also facilitated Continuing Profes-
sional Development (CPD)-accredited training interven-
tions for employees of medical schemes, administrators, 
healthcare service providers, and other industry stake-
holders. These programmes were offered at a nominal 
fee to recover operational costs and focused on enhanc-
ing the understanding and application of PMBs, includ-
ing both general PMB requirements and specific dental 
PMB conditions. 
Through these initiatives, the CMS continues to pro-
mote regulatory compliance, informed decision-making, 
and improved stakeholder capacity across the medical 
schemes environment.

CMS Communications
The Communication Unit plays a strategic role in support-
ing the CMS mandate through effective communication, 
stakeholder engagement, and public awareness initia-
tives. During the 2025/26 financial year, the unit used a 
range of digital, traditional, and internal communication 
channels to provide stakeholders with timely access to 
regulatory information, consumer education resources, 
and organisational updates. These efforts contributed 
to an increased visibility of the CMS and it strengthened 
stakeholder engagement. It also supported the organi-
sation’s commitment to transparency and consumer em-
powerment.
Digital Media 
Website
As far as digital communication is concerned, the CMS 
website continued to serve as a key information and 
stakeholder engagement platform during the reporting 
period. The website records an average of 45 000 page 
views from 15 000 active users per month. Publications, 
Prescribed Minimum Benefits information, and consumer 
education resources were among the most accessed con-
tent areas, demonstrating strong stakeholder demand for 
regulatory information, healthcare benefits guidance, and 
career opportunities.
The engagement remained positive, with visitors viewing 
an average of three pages per session and spending an 
average of 1 minute and 43 seconds on the website.
Email Communication
Email communication remained an important channel 
for disseminating regulatory information, consumer edu-
cation content, stakeholder updates, and organisational 
publications during the 2025/26 financial year.
The unit used Mailchimp to distribute 47 email campaigns 
to a subscriber base of 50 427 stakeholders. During the 
reporting period, the mailing list experienced significant 
growth through a collaborative initiative involving the 
Communications, Complaints, and Information and Com-
munication Technology (ICT) Units. As part of this initia-
tive, the complaints process was updated to include a 
POPIA-compliant opt-in mechanism, enabling complain-
ants to voluntarily subscribe to CMS publications and 
updates. This approach has expanded the CMS’ 
communication reach while ensuring the responsible and 
transparent use of personal information.
The email communication platform generated a total of 1 
730 895 email sends during the year, achieving a delivery 
rate of 98.58%, which reflects the quality and reliability 
of the CMS subscriber database. Campaigns recorded 
an average open rate of 28.43%, demonstrating strong 
stakeholder engagement and aligning with industry 
benchmarks for government and non-profit organisations. 
To further improve engagement, the team introduced 
Mailchimp’s resend functionality, which automatically re-
sends selected campaigns to subscribers who did not 
open the original email, contributing to increased open 
and click-through rates.
The continued growth of the subscriber base and strong 
engagement levels demonstrate the effectiveness of email 
communication. It is a strategic tool for increasing 
awareness, promoting stakeholder participation and 
ensuring timely access to the CMS’ information and 
publications.
Social Media 
The Unit continued to strengthen the CMS’ digital 
communication and stakeholder engagement efforts 
during the 2025/26 financial year. This happened through 
its presence on social media platforms and the introduction 
of a new WhatsApp communication channel.
The CMS maintains active accounts on the following social 
media platforms:
During the year under review, the CMS published a total 
of 559 social media posts, achieving a cumulative reach 
of 52.6 million users across all platforms. X remained the 
most prominent platform, accounting for 53% of the over-
all share of voice, followed by LinkedIn (29%), Facebook 
(13%) and YouTube (5%).
Analysis of online sentiment reflected largely neutral to 
positive stakeholder engagement, with 370 (66%) neutral 
mentions, 144 (26%) positive mentions, and 45 (8%) nega-
tive mentions recorded across social media platforms.

The continued growth of the CMS’ digital footprint has 
enhanced its ability to communicate with stakeholders in

WhatsApp Channel
In a move to expand access to information and meet 
stakeholders on the platforms that they use most frequent-
ly, the CMS launched its official WhatsApp Channel on 13 
October 2025. The introduction of the channel represents 
an innovative approach to stakeholder communication 
within the healthcare regulatory environment. It leverages 
one of South Africa’s most widely used messaging plat-
forms to deliver timely, accessible, and mobile-friendly 
information directly to subscribers. The channel also pro-
vides an alternative communication platform for stake-
holders who may not consistently receive email commu-
nications due to organisational email filtering, mailbox 
restrictions or other technical limitations.
The WhatsApp Channel provides stakeholders with easy 
access to key CMS information, including:
• Circulars, CMScripts, and other regulatory publica-
tions.
• Announcements on member education initiatives and 
training sessions.
• Updates on the CMS Annual Report, the Industry Re-
port, and other strategic publications.
• Vacancy announcements and organisational updates.
Since its launch, the channel has attracted 845 followers, 
and the content shared through the channel during the 
reporting period included:
• 29 CMS Circulars
• 6 CMScripts
• 1 CMS Annual Report
• 1 CMS Industry Report
• 1 Seasonal greetings message
• 13 Member Education posts
• 4 Vacancy announcements
• 2 Survey notices
• 40 CMS Industry Indaba updates
The channel achieved a reach of 479 users, of which 95.4% 
were followers and 4.6% were non-followers, demonstrat-
ing strong engagement among subscribers. While fol-
lower growth remained relatively stable during the report-
ing period, the channel continues to establish itself as a 
valuable communication tool for delivering regulatory in-
formation and stakeholder updates directly to the public.
Overall, the CMS’ growing digital presence has enabled 
more effective, timely, and accessible engagement with 
industry stakeholders and the members whose interests 
the CMS protects. Through an integrated approach that 
combines social media, digital publications, and the 
innovative WhatsApp Channel, the Communications 
Unit continues to expand the reach of its consumer edu-
cation initiatives. It also attempts to strengthen transpar -
ency and support its strategic objective of increasing 
public awareness and stakeholder engagement.
Traditional Media Coverage
The CMS also continued to maintain a strong presence 
across traditional media platforms, reflecting the Unit’s 
efforts to raise public awareness and communicate reg-
ulatory developments. During the year under review, the 
CMS generated a total of 1 404 media mentions across 
online, broadcast, and print media platforms. This cover-
age achieved a cumulative reach of 1.02 billion and an 
Advertising Value Equivalent (AVE) of R48.2 million, re-
flecting increased visibility of the regulator and its activi-
ties in the media landscape.
Online media generated the highest volume of coverage, 
followed by broadcast and print media. This trend reflects 
the growing importance of digital news platforms as a pri-
mary source of information for stakeholders and the pub-
lic. Media sentiment analysis indicated that the majority 
of coverage was neutral in tone, with 818 mentions (58%) 
classified as neutral, 150 mentions (11%) as positive, and 
436 mentions (31%) as negative. The predominance of 
neutral coverage demonstrates the media’s continued use 
of the CMS as a credible source of information and com-
mentary on developments within the medical schemes 
sector.
X dominated social media coverage in the 2025/26 year compared to LinkedIn, Facebook and YouTube.

Internal Communication
Internal communication continued to play a key role in 
maintaining an informed, engaged, and connected 
workforce during the 2025/26 financial year. The 
Communications Unit supported organisational cohesion 
by ensuring that employees received timely information 
on strategic priorities, organisational developments, 
employee initiatives, and key operational updates.
A variety of communication channels were used to reach 
employees, including staff-wide email communications, 
WhatsApp announcements and the Masihambisane in-
ternal newsletter. During the reporting period, the Unit 
distributed 226 internal email communications to keep 
employees informed of organisational developments and 
key initiatives. Regular Ethics Bulletins were distributed to 
reinforce organisational values, ethical conduct and the 
commemoration of significant national observances. The 
Communications Unit also coordinated the dissemination 
of internal notices and memoranda to keep employees 
informed of policies, governance matters, and organisa-
tional initiatives.
Through these efforts, internal communication strength-
ened employee engagement, supported alignment with 
the CMS’ strategic objectives and contributed to a trans-
parent and values-driven organisational culture.
Awareness Survey
The annual Council for Medical Schemes (CMS) Aware-
ness Survey was conducted from 9 March 2026 to 14 
April 2026. It was executed through the CMS web-
site, email mailers, social media platforms, and letters 
distributed to medical scheme principal officers. The 
survey forms part of the CMS’ efforts to monitor public 
awareness of the organisation and its mandate. The 
CMS set target of achieving 65% awareness among 
respondents during the 2025/26 financial year.
To enable more meaningful measurement of awareness 
levels, the CMS continued to use the familiarity scale 
introduced in the previous reporting period. Respondents 
were asked to indicate whether they were very familiar, 
familiar, not so familiar or not at all familiar with the CMS. 
This provided deeper insight into levels of awareness and 
understanding of the organisation.
A total of 19 078 respondents participated in the survey. 
Of these, 8.63% indicated that they were very familiar with 
the CMS, 20.85% were familiar, 44.16% were not 
so familiar, and 26.36% were not at all familiar. Based on 
the CMS methodology, respondents who indicated that 
they were very familiar, familiar, or not so familiar, with the 
organisation were considered aware of the CMS.
The survey recorded an overall awareness level of 73.64%, 
exceeding the annual target of 65% and reflecting an 
improvement from the 69.94% recorded in the previous 
reporting period. The increase in awareness may be at-
tributed to ongoing consumer education initiatives, tar -
geted communication campaigns, and stakeholder 
engagement activities undertaken throughout the year.
Stakeholder Engagement 
Stakeholder engagement remains a critical component 
of effective regulation, enabling the CMS to promote 
transparency, strengthen collaboration and ensure that 
regulatory interventions are informed by stakeholder per-
spectives. The Communications Unit therefore maintains 
ongoing engagement with medical schemes, healthcare 
providers, industry associations, government stakehold-
ers and other key role players, supporting constructive 
dialogue on matters affecting the medical schemes envi-
ronment and the broader private healthcare sector.
During the 2025/26 financial year, the CMS hosted and 
participated in several stakeholder engagements aimed at 
advancing regulatory priorities and addressing emerging 
industry matters. A key milestone was the launch of the 
Healthcare Utilisation and Financial Performance Report, 
marking the first full and timely release of this important 
industry publication since 2022.
Section 59
The implementation of section 59 reforms remains one of 
the most significant regulatory interventions undertaken 
by the CMS in recent years. Following extensive stake-
holder engagement, independent investigations, parlia-
mentary oversight processes and the publication of the

Facilities Management
Facilities Management is responsible for ensuring that the 
CMS facilities remain functional, accessible, well-main-
tained, and safe. Its core functions include the mainte-
nance and upkeep of the building and infrastructure, the 
provision of cleaning services, the maintenance of 
external grounds and gardens, and fleet manage-
ment. The latter currently consists of one pool car used 
for official CMS operations.
During the reporting period, the Unit continued to support 
daily operational requirements by ensuring a clean, safe, 
and functional working environment. Routine maintenance 
and cleaning services were rendered to maintain service 
continuity and uphold workplace standards.
Highlights
An achievement during the reporting period was the 
successful completion of the external building renovations 
in December 2025. This strengthened the condition, 
functionality, and professional image of the CMS’ premises 
while supporting long-term asset preservation.
Furthermore, the Facilities Management Unit con-
ducted a survey on the prevalence of electric vehicles 
among CMS internal stakeholders. This has led to the 
development of the draft Electric Vehicle Policy, which 
will inform future engagements with the landlord on 
the feasibility of an electric vehicle-charging facility and 
financing thereof.
The Unit continued to work closely with the landlord and 
service providers to ensure the effective delivery of main-
tenance, repairs, security, landscaping, waste manage-
ment, and other facility-related services. Through regular 
engagement and performance monitoring, the Unit en-
sured that services were delivered in accordance with or-
ganisational requirements and service-level expectations.
The Unit also reinforced the CMS’ environmental sustain-
ability agenda through targeted energy-saving measures, 
including reducing non-essential lighting after business 
hours. Quarterly environmental responsibility reports 
were submitted to the Social and Ethics Committee, pro-
viding governance oversight and supporting the continu-
ous monitoring of environmental performance.
The Unit’s performance was further built on the successful 
insourcing of cleaning services, which enhanced service 
consistency, strengthened internal capacity, and support-
ed organisational stability through the permanent ap-
pointment of cleaning personnel.
Looking ahead, the Facilities Management Unit remains 
focused on sustaining fit-for-purpose, efficient, and envi-
ronmentally responsible facilities that support the CMS’ 
mandate, workforce, and stakeholder experience.
Section 59 Inquiry Report, CMS has led a comprehensive 
programme of regulatory reform aimed at eradicating un-
fair discrimination and ensuring that members and bene-
ficiaries are treated with fairness, dignity and procedural 
justice. 
CMS’ work has extended beyond regulatory enforce-
ment to include the development of a robust implemen-
tation framework, continuous engagement with medical 
schemes and administrators, the establishment of report-
ing and monitoring mechanisms, and the promotion of 
systemic changes designed to eliminate practices that 
may result in indirect discrimination. Through this process, 
CMS has reaffirmed its statutory mandate to protect the 
interests of beneficiaries and to ensure that the medical 
schemes environment operates in a manner that is trans-
parent, equitable and consistent with the values of the 
Constitution.
Health Market Inquiry
CMS’ implementation programme has also been in-
formed by broader competition and market reform ini-
tiatives arising from the Health Market Inquiry (HMI). 
The findings of the HMI underscored the importance of 
transparency, accountability, good governance and fair 
market conduct within the private healthcare sector. CMS 
has therefore sought to align its section 59 reform agen-
da with wider efforts to strengthen consumer protection, 
improve accountability within funding arrangements and 
promote public confidence in the medical schemes indus-
try. Through collaboration with industry stakeholders, reg-
ulators, policymakers and consumer representatives, CMS 
has continued to drive reforms that support an efficient, 
competitive and member-centred healthcare financing 
environment. These initiatives collectively demonstrate 
the CMS’ commitment to transforming regulatory over -
sight into tangible protections for beneficiaries while con-
tributing to a more equitable and sustainable health sys-
tem for South Africa.
The Communications Unit also facilitated two Fraud, 
Waste and Abuse (FWA) Advisory Committee meetings, 
contributing to ongoing efforts to strengthen collabo-
ration in addressing fraud, waste and abuse within the 
medical schemes environment. These engagements also 
informed preparations for the CMS Industry Indaba 2026, 
which would provide a broader platform for dialogue on 
key regulatory and industry matters.
Overall, stakeholder engagement during the 2025/26 fi-
nancial year supported the CMS’ efforts to strengthen dia-
logue with the sector, communicate significant regulatory 
developments and facilitate stakeholder participation on 
matters affecting medical scheme beneficiaries and regu-
lated entities. Together with the CMS’ digital, media and 
internal communication activities, these engagements 
contributed to greater awareness of the CMS’ mandate 
and regulatory priorities.
